Arizona Grade 6 Mathematics

140 Day Editable Curriculum Master. Original lessons and assessments. Expand answer sections for teacher use. Print student PDFs for predictable work space.

Day 1 — Unit 1 Whole Numbers and Decimals

Lesson 1 Long Division and Place Value

Standards: 6.NS.B.2

Learning target: I can divide multi-digit whole numbers and check the quotient.

Estimate the quotient first. Divide, multiply, subtract, and bring down one digit at a time. Keep each quotient digit in its place; write zero when needed. Check dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder.

Worked example

Use long division: 138 ÷ 6. Show the algorithm and check.

23 13 ÷ 6: write 2, subtract 12, remainder 1; bring down the next digit: 18 ÷ 6: write 3, subtract 18, remainder 0. Check: 6 × 23 = 138.

Worked example

Use long division: 12,183 ÷ 34. Show the algorithm and check.

358 R 11 121 ÷ 34: write 3, subtract 102, remainder 19; bring down the next digit: 198 ÷ 34: write 5, subtract 170, remainder 28; bring down the next digit: 283 ÷ 34: write 8, subtract 272, remainder 11. Check: 34 × 358 + 11 = 12183.

  1. Use long division: 438 ÷ 6. Show the algorithm and check.
  2. Use long division: 175 ÷ 7. Show the algorithm and check.
  3. Use long division: 252 ÷ 6. Show the algorithm and check.
  4. Use long division: 14,174 ÷ 38. Show the algorithm and check.
  5. Use long division: 3,388 ÷ 14. Show the algorithm and check.
  6. Use long division: 12,408 ÷ 33. Show the algorithm and check.
  7. Use long division: 5,492 ÷ 29. Show the algorithm and check.
  8. Use long division: 5,575 ÷ 21. Show the algorithm and check.
  9. A club has 2351 cards. Each full packet holds 15. How many full packets can it make, and how many cards remain?
  10. A club has 3245 cards. Each full packet holds 12. How many full packets can it make, and how many cards remain?
Teacher answer key
  1. 73
    43 ÷ 6: write 7, subtract 42, remainder 1; bring down the next digit: 18 ÷ 6: write 3, subtract 18, remainder 0. Check: 6 × 73 = 438.
    6.NS.B.2
  2. 25
    17 ÷ 7: write 2, subtract 14, remainder 3; bring down the next digit: 35 ÷ 7: write 5, subtract 35, remainder 0. Check: 7 × 25 = 175.
    6.NS.B.2
  3. 42
    25 ÷ 6: write 4, subtract 24, remainder 1; bring down the next digit: 12 ÷ 6: write 2, subtract 12, remainder 0. Check: 6 × 42 = 252.
    6.NS.B.2
  4. 373
    141 ÷ 38: write 3, subtract 114, remainder 27; bring down the next digit: 277 ÷ 38: write 7, subtract 266, remainder 11; bring down the next digit: 114 ÷ 38: write 3, subtract 114, remainder 0. Check: 38 × 373 = 14174.
    6.NS.B.2
  5. 242
    33 ÷ 14: write 2, subtract 28, remainder 5; bring down the next digit: 58 ÷ 14: write 4, subtract 56, remainder 2; bring down the next digit: 28 ÷ 14: write 2, subtract 28, remainder 0. Check: 14 × 242 = 3388.
    6.NS.B.2
  6. 376
    124 ÷ 33: write 3, subtract 99, remainder 25; bring down the next digit: 250 ÷ 33: write 7, subtract 231, remainder 19; bring down the next digit: 198 ÷ 33: write 6, subtract 198, remainder 0. Check: 33 × 376 = 12408.
    6.NS.B.2
  7. 189 R 11
    54 ÷ 29: write 1, subtract 29, remainder 25; bring down the next digit: 259 ÷ 29: write 8, subtract 232, remainder 27; bring down the next digit: 272 ÷ 29: write 9, subtract 261, remainder 11. Check: 29 × 189 + 11 = 5492.
    6.NS.B.2
  8. 265 R 10
    55 ÷ 21: write 2, subtract 42, remainder 13; bring down the next digit: 137 ÷ 21: write 6, subtract 126, remainder 11; bring down the next digit: 115 ÷ 21: write 5, subtract 105, remainder 10. Check: 21 × 265 + 10 = 5575.
    6.NS.B.2
  9. 156 full packets; 11 cards remain
    2351 = 15 × 156 + 11.
    6.NS.B.2
  10. 270 full packets; 5 cards remain
    3245 = 12 × 270 + 5.
    6.NS.B.2

Day 2 — Unit 1 Whole Numbers and Decimals

Lesson 2 Quotients with Zero Digits

Standards: 6.NS.B.2

Learning target: I can divide multi-digit whole numbers and check the quotient.

Estimate the quotient first. Divide, multiply, subtract, and bring down one digit at a time. Keep each quotient digit in its place; write zero when needed. Check dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder.

Worked example

Use long division: 603 ÷ 3. Show the algorithm and check.

201 6 ÷ 3: write 2, subtract 6, remainder 0; bring down the next digit: 0 ÷ 3: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 0; bring down the next digit: 3 ÷ 3: write 1, subtract 3, remainder 0. Check: 3 × 201 = 603.

Worked example

Use long division: 8,752 ÷ 29. Show the algorithm and check.

301 R 23 87 ÷ 29: write 3, subtract 87, remainder 0; bring down the next digit: 5 ÷ 29: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 5; bring down the next digit: 52 ÷ 29: write 1, subtract 29, remainder 23. Check: 29 × 301 + 23 = 8752.

  1. Use long division: 728 ÷ 7. Show the algorithm and check.
  2. Use long division: 903 ÷ 3. Show the algorithm and check.
  3. Use long division: 420 ÷ 4. Show the algorithm and check.
  4. Use long division: 7,218 ÷ 18. Show the algorithm and check.
  5. Use long division: 2,484 ÷ 23. Show the algorithm and check.
  6. Use long division: 1,744 ÷ 16. Show the algorithm and check.
  7. Use long division: 10,696 ÷ 35. Show the algorithm and check.
  8. Use long division: 2,311 ÷ 22. Show the algorithm and check.
  9. A club has 4751 cards. Each full packet holds 23. How many full packets can it make, and how many cards remain?
  10. A club has 9530 cards. Each full packet holds 31. How many full packets can it make, and how many cards remain?
Teacher answer key
  1. 104
    7 ÷ 7: write 1, subtract 7, remainder 0; bring down the next digit: 2 ÷ 7: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 2; bring down the next digit: 28 ÷ 7: write 4, subtract 28, remainder 0. Check: 7 × 104 = 728.
    6.NS.B.2
  2. 301
    9 ÷ 3: write 3, subtract 9, remainder 0; bring down the next digit: 0 ÷ 3: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 0; bring down the next digit: 3 ÷ 3: write 1, subtract 3, remainder 0. Check: 3 × 301 = 903.
    6.NS.B.2
  3. 105
    4 ÷ 4: write 1, subtract 4, remainder 0; bring down the next digit: 2 ÷ 4: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 2; bring down the next digit: 20 ÷ 4: write 5, subtract 20, remainder 0. Check: 4 × 105 = 420.
    6.NS.B.2
  4. 401
    72 ÷ 18: write 4, subtract 72, remainder 0; bring down the next digit: 1 ÷ 18: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 1; bring down the next digit: 18 ÷ 18: write 1, subtract 18, remainder 0. Check: 18 × 401 = 7218.
    6.NS.B.2
  5. 108
    24 ÷ 23: write 1, subtract 23, remainder 1; bring down the next digit: 18 ÷ 23: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 18; bring down the next digit: 184 ÷ 23: write 8, subtract 184, remainder 0. Check: 23 × 108 = 2484.
    6.NS.B.2
  6. 109
    17 ÷ 16: write 1, subtract 16, remainder 1; bring down the next digit: 14 ÷ 16: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 14; bring down the next digit: 144 ÷ 16: write 9, subtract 144, remainder 0. Check: 16 × 109 = 1744.
    6.NS.B.2
  7. 305 R 21
    106 ÷ 35: write 3, subtract 105, remainder 1; bring down the next digit: 19 ÷ 35: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 19; bring down the next digit: 196 ÷ 35: write 5, subtract 175, remainder 21. Check: 35 × 305 + 21 = 10696.
    6.NS.B.2
  8. 105 R 1
    23 ÷ 22: write 1, subtract 22, remainder 1; bring down the next digit: 11 ÷ 22: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 11; bring down the next digit: 111 ÷ 22: write 5, subtract 110, remainder 1. Check: 22 × 105 + 1 = 2311.
    6.NS.B.2
  9. 206 full packets; 13 cards remain
    4751 = 23 × 206 + 13.
    6.NS.B.2
  10. 307 full packets; 13 cards remain
    9530 = 31 × 307 + 13.
    6.NS.B.2

Day 3 — Unit 1 Whole Numbers and Decimals

Lesson 3 Adding Decimals

Standards: 6.NS.B.3

Learning target: I can add decimals by aligning equal place values.

Line up decimal points so tenths are under tenths and hundredths under hundredths. Add zeros if helpful. Add from right to left, regroup, and bring the decimal point straight down.

Worked example

Use the standard algorithm: 19.1 + 2.6. Show your regrouping or division steps.

21.7 Align decimal points; 19.1 + 2.6 = 21.7.

Worked example

Use the standard algorithm: 6.2 + 0.4. Show your regrouping or division steps.

6.6 Align decimal points; 6.2 + 0.4 = 6.6.

  1. Use the standard algorithm: 76.1 + 4.8. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  2. Use the standard algorithm: 63.1 + 4.4. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  3. Use the standard algorithm: 82.5 + 7.1. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  4. Use the standard algorithm: 3 + 4.3. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  5. Use the standard algorithm: 5.86 + 3.7. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  6. Use the standard algorithm: 7.01 + 8.8. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  7. Use the standard algorithm: 8.94 + 0.69. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  8. Use the standard algorithm: 6.68 + 0.52. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  9. A runner walks 8.36 km in the morning and 0.82 km later. What is the total distance?
  10. A runner walks 5.45 km in the morning and 0.61 km later. What is the total distance?
Teacher answer key
  1. 80.9
    Align decimal points; 76.1 + 4.8 = 80.9.
    6.NS.B.3
  2. 67.5
    Align decimal points; 63.1 + 4.4 = 67.5.
    6.NS.B.3
  3. 89.6
    Align decimal points; 82.5 + 7.1 = 89.6.
    6.NS.B.3
  4. 7.3
    Align decimal points; 3 + 4.3 = 7.3.
    6.NS.B.3
  5. 9.56
    Align decimal points; 5.86 + 3.7 = 9.56.
    6.NS.B.3
  6. 15.81
    Align decimal points; 7.01 + 8.8 = 15.81.
    6.NS.B.3
  7. 9.63
    Align decimal points; 8.94 + 0.69 = 9.63.
    6.NS.B.3
  8. 7.2
    Align decimal points; 6.68 + 0.52 = 7.2.
    6.NS.B.3
  9. 9.18 km
    Align decimal points; 8.36 + 0.82 = 9.18.
    6.NS.B.3
  10. 6.06 km
    Align decimal points; 5.45 + 0.61 = 6.06.
    6.NS.B.3

Day 4 — Unit 1 Whole Numbers and Decimals

Lesson 4 Subtracting Decimals

Standards: 6.NS.B.3

Learning target: I can subtract decimals and explain regrouping.

Align decimal points and add placeholder zeros. Regroup one whole as ten tenths, or one tenth as ten hundredths. Subtract by place and check by adding the difference to the subtrahend.

Worked example

Use the standard algorithm: 88.6 - 8.8. Show your regrouping or division steps.

79.8 Align decimal points; 88.6 - 8.8 = 79.8.

Worked example

Use the standard algorithm: 3.22 - 0.48. Show your regrouping or division steps.

2.74 Align decimal points; 3.22 - 0.48 = 2.74.

  1. Use the standard algorithm: 65.1 - 7.8. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  2. Use the standard algorithm: 83.1 - 5.3. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  3. Use the standard algorithm: 21 - 1.5. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  4. Use the standard algorithm: 6 - 3.77. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  5. Use the standard algorithm: 8.13 - 6.6. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  6. Use the standard algorithm: 4.25 - 3.3. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  7. Use the standard algorithm: 3.27 - 0.44. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  8. Use the standard algorithm: 9.14 - 0.87. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  9. A tank holds 7.85 L. A class uses 0.15 L. How much remains?
  10. A tank holds 4.73 L. A class uses 0.79 L. How much remains?
Teacher answer key
  1. 57.3
    Align decimal points; 65.1 - 7.8 = 57.3.
    6.NS.B.3
  2. 77.8
    Align decimal points; 83.1 - 5.3 = 77.8.
    6.NS.B.3
  3. 19.5
    Align decimal points; 21 - 1.5 = 19.5.
    6.NS.B.3
  4. 2.23
    Align decimal points; 6 - 3.77 = 2.23.
    6.NS.B.3
  5. 1.53
    Align decimal points; 8.13 - 6.6 = 1.53.
    6.NS.B.3
  6. 0.95
    Align decimal points; 4.25 - 3.3 = 0.95.
    6.NS.B.3
  7. 2.83
    Align decimal points; 3.27 - 0.44 = 2.83.
    6.NS.B.3
  8. 8.27
    Align decimal points; 9.14 - 0.87 = 8.27.
    6.NS.B.3
  9. 7.7 L
    Align decimal points; 7.85 - 0.15 = 7.7.
    6.NS.B.3
  10. 3.94 L
    Align decimal points; 4.73 - 0.79 = 3.94.
    6.NS.B.3

Day 5 — Unit 1 Whole Numbers and Decimals

Quiz Quiz 1

Standards: Grade 5 prerequisite, 6.NS.B.2, 6.NS.B.3

Learning target: I can apply the skills taught so far, show my reasoning, and check my answers.

The first 1 questions review Grade 5 prerequisites.

  1. Use long division: 120 ÷ 5. Show the algorithm and check.
  2. Use long division: 8,296 ÷ 34. Show the algorithm and check.
  3. Use long division: 4,298 ÷ 14. Show the algorithm and check.
  4. Use the standard algorithm: 3.39 + 0.7. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  5. A tank holds 1.59 L. A class uses 0.43 L. How much remains?
Teacher answer key
  1. 24
    12 ÷ 5: write 2, subtract 10, remainder 2; bring down the next digit: 20 ÷ 5: write 4, subtract 20, remainder 0. Check: 5 × 24 = 120.
    Grade 5 prerequisite
  2. 244
    82 ÷ 34: write 2, subtract 68, remainder 14; bring down the next digit: 149 ÷ 34: write 4, subtract 136, remainder 13; bring down the next digit: 136 ÷ 34: write 4, subtract 136, remainder 0. Check: 34 × 244 = 8296.
    6.NS.B.2
  3. 307
    42 ÷ 14: write 3, subtract 42, remainder 0; bring down the next digit: 9 ÷ 14: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 9; bring down the next digit: 98 ÷ 14: write 7, subtract 98, remainder 0. Check: 14 × 307 = 4298.
    6.NS.B.2
  4. 4.09
    Align decimal points; 3.39 + 0.7 = 4.09.
    6.NS.B.3
  5. 1.16 L
    Align decimal points; 1.59 - 0.43 = 1.16.
    6.NS.B.3

Day 6 — Unit 1 Whole Numbers and Decimals

Lesson 5 Multiplying Decimals

Standards: 6.NS.B.3

Learning target: I can multiply decimals and use an estimate to place the decimal point.

Multiply as whole numbers using the standard algorithm. The product has the total number of decimal places in the two factors before trailing zeros are removed. Check its size against an estimate.

Worked example

Use the standard algorithm: 17.7 × 7.6. Show your regrouping or division steps.

134.52 Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 17.7 × 7.6 = 134.52.

Worked example

Use the standard algorithm: 4.28 × 0.77. Show your regrouping or division steps.

3.2956 Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 4.28 × 0.77 = 3.2956.

  1. Use the standard algorithm: 39.7 × 5.2. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  2. Use the standard algorithm: 29.4 × 3.9. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  3. Use the standard algorithm: 68.4 × 1.3. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  4. Use the standard algorithm: 4.86 × 5.2. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  5. Use the standard algorithm: 3.5 × 3.9. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  6. Use the standard algorithm: 8.93 × 2.1. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  7. Use the standard algorithm: 2.54 × 0.26. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  8. Use the standard algorithm: 7.49 × 0.21. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  9. Ribbon costs $7.16 per meter. What is the exact cost of 0.51 meters? Do not round.
  10. Ribbon costs $1.42 per meter. What is the exact cost of 0.5 meters? Do not round.
Teacher answer key
  1. 206.44
    Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 39.7 × 5.2 = 206.44.

  2. 114.66
    Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 29.4 × 3.9 = 114.66.
    6.NS.B.3
  3. 88.92
    Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 68.4 × 1.3 = 88.92.
    6.NS.B.3
  4. 25.272
    Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 4.86 × 5.2 = 25.272.
    6.NS.B.3
  5. 13.65
    Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 3.5 × 3.9 = 13.65.
    6.NS.B.3
  6. 18.753
    Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 8.93 × 2.1 = 18.753.
    6.NS.B.3
  7. 0.6604
    Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 2.54 × 0.26 = 0.6604.
    6.NS.B.3
  8. 1.5729
    Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 7.49 × 0.21 = 1.5729.
    6.NS.B.3
  9. 3.6516 dollars

    6.NS.B.3

    Compute the whole-number product and restore decimal places: 1.42 × 0.5 = 0.71.

Day 8 — Unit 1 Whole Numbers and Decimals

Lesson 7 Dividing by Decimals

Standards: 6.NS.B.3

Learning target: I can divide by a decimal using an equivalent whole-number divisor.

Multiply both dividend and divisor by the same power of 10 so the divisor is a whole number. Then use long division, adding zeros to the dividend as needed. Check by multiplication.

Worked example

Use the standard algorithm: 34.5 ÷ 0.5. Show your regrouping or division steps.

69 Multiply both numbers by 10: 345 ÷ 5 = 69. Check: 0.5 × 69 = 34.5.

Worked example

Use the standard algorithm: 2.496 ÷ 0.32. Show your regrouping or division steps.

7.8 Multiply both numbers by 100: 249.6 ÷ 32 = 7.8. Check: 0.32 × 7.8 = 2.496.

  1. Use the standard algorithm: 8.1 ÷ 0.3. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  2. Use the standard algorithm: 60 ÷ 0.8. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  3. Use the standard algorithm: 21 ÷ 0.7. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  4. Use the standard algorithm: 18.24 ÷ 2.4. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  5. Use the standard algorithm: 12.72 ÷ 2.4. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  6. Use the standard algorithm: 47.36 ÷ 6.4. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  7. Use the standard algorithm: 0.608 ÷ 0.32. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  8. Use the standard algorithm: 0.64 ÷ 0.32. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  9. A container has 0.928 kg of seed. Each bag holds 0.32 kg. How many bags can be filled?
  10. A container has 2.016 kg of seed. Each bag holds 0.24 kg. How many bags can be filled?
Teacher answer key
  1. 27
    Multiply both numbers by 10: 81 ÷ 3 = 27. Check: 0.3 × 27 = 8.1.
    6.NS.B.3
  2. 75
    Multiply both numbers by 10: 600 ÷ 8 = 75. Check: 0.8 × 75 = 60.
    6.NS.B.3
  3. 30
    Multiply both numbers by 10: 210 ÷ 7 = 30. Check: 0.7 × 30 = 21.
    6.NS.B.3
  4. 7.6
    Multiply both numbers by 10: 182.4 ÷ 24 = 7.6. Check: 2.4 × 7.6 = 18.24.
    6.NS.B.3
  5. 5.3
    Multiply both numbers by 10: 127.2 ÷ 24 = 5.3. Check: 2.4 × 5.3 = 12.72.
    6.NS.B.3
  6. 7.4
    Multiply both numbers by 10: 473.6 ÷ 64 = 7.4. Check: 6.4 × 7.4 = 47.36.
    6.NS.B.3
  7. 1.9
    Multiply both numbers by 100: 60.8 ÷ 32 = 1.9. Check: 0.32 × 1.9 = 0.608.
    6.NS.B.3
  8. 2
    Multiply both numbers by 100: 64 ÷ 32 = 2. Check: 0.32 × 2 = 0.64.
    6.NS.B.3
  9. 2.9 bags
    Multiply both numbers by 100: 92.8 ÷ 32 = 2.9. Check: 0.32 × 2.9 = 0.928.
    6.NS.B.3
  10. 8.4 bags
    Multiply both numbers by 100: 201.6 ÷ 24 = 8.4. Check: 0.24 × 8.4 = 2.016.
    6.NS.B.3

Day 9 — Unit 1 Whole Numbers and Decimals

Lesson 8 Decimal Quotients and Checks

Standards: 6.NS.B.3

Learning target: I can divide by a decimal using an equivalent whole-number divisor.

Multiply both dividend and divisor by the same power of 10 so the divisor is a whole number. Then use long division, adding zeros to the dividend as needed. Check by multiplication.

Worked example

Use the standard algorithm: 50.4 ÷ 0.7. Show your regrouping or division steps.

72 Multiply both numbers by 10: 504 ÷ 7 = 72. Check: 0.7 × 72 = 50.4.

Worked example

Use the standard algorithm: 2.92 ÷ 0.4. Show your regrouping or division steps.

7.3 Multiply both numbers by 10: 29.2 ÷ 4 = 7.3. Check: 0.4 × 7.3 = 2.92.

  1. Use the standard algorithm: 40.5 ÷ 0.9. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  2. Use the standard algorithm: 20.1 ÷ 0.3. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  3. Use the standard algorithm: 23.7 ÷ 0.3. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  4. Use the standard algorithm: 10 ÷ 2.5. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  5. Use the standard algorithm: 13.68 ÷ 2.4. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  6. Use the standard algorithm: 6 ÷ 2.4. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  7. Use the standard algorithm: 0.825 ÷ 0.15. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  8. Use the standard algorithm: 0.8 ÷ 0.25. Show your regrouping or division steps.
  9. A container has 2.368 kg of seed. Each bag holds 0.64 kg. How many bags can be filled?
  10. A container has 0.768 kg of seed. Each bag holds 0.24 kg. How many bags can be filled?
Teacher answer key
  1. 45
    Multiply both numbers by 10: 405 ÷ 9 = 45. Check: 0.9 × 45 = 40.5.
    6.NS.B.3
  2. 67
    Multiply both numbers by 10: 201 ÷ 3 = 67. Check: 0.3 × 67 = 20.1.
    6.NS.B.3
  3. 79
    Multiply both numbers by 10: 237 ÷ 3 = 79. Check: 0.3 × 79 = 23.7.
    6.NS.B.3

Day 11 — Unit 1 Whole Numbers and Decimals

Lesson 9 Greatest Common Factors

Standards: 6.NS.B.4a

Learning target: I can find the greatest common factor and interpret equal groups.

List factor pairs or use prime factorizations. The greatest factor shared by both numbers is the GCF. For identical kits with no leftovers, the GCF gives the greatest number of kits.

Worked example

Find the GCF of 12 and 16. Show factor pairs or prime factors.

4 Common factors: 1, 2, 4; greatest is 4.

Worked example

Make the greatest possible number of identical kits from 18 pencils and 30 erasers with no leftovers. Find the number of kits and each kit’s contents.

6 kits; 3 pencils and 5 erasers each GCF(18, 30) = 6; divide both counts by 6.

Lesson 11 Factoring Sums

Standards: 6.NS.B.4c

Learning target: I can factor a sum using the greatest common factor.

Find the GCF of the two addends. Divide each addend by that factor and place the quotients inside parentheses. The inside numbers should share no common factor greater than 1.

Worked example

Factor 10 + 15 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

5(2 + 3) GCF = 5; 10 = 5 × 2 and 15 = 5 × 3.

Worked example

Factor 18 + 30 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

6(3 + 5) GCF = 6; 18 = 6 × 3 and 30 = 6 × 5.

Lesson 12 Division with Remainders in Context

Standards: 6.NS.B.2

Learning target: I can divide multi-digit whole numbers and check the quotient.

Estimate the quotient first. Divide, multiply, subtract, and bring down one digit at a time. Keep each quotient digit in its place; write zero when needed. Check dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder.

Worked example

Use long division: 90 ÷ 3. Show the algorithm and check.

30 9 ÷ 3: write 3, subtract 9, remainder 0; bring down the next digit: 0 ÷ 3: write 0, subtract 0, remainder 0. Check: 3 × 30 = 90.

Day 18 — Unit 2 Fraction Operations and Models

Lesson 3 Equal Groups in Fraction Bars

Standards: 6.NS.A.1

Learning target: I can model fraction division as equal-sized groups.

A quotient asks how many groups of the divisor fit in the dividend. Rename both fractions with a common denominator, draw equal parts, and count groups. A partial group is a fraction of the divisor, not of the original whole.

Worked example

Draw a bar partitioned into 10 equal parts. Shade 6 parts. Circle groups of 4 parts. Use the model to find 3/5 ÷ 2/5.

3/2 Each small part is 1/10; 6 shaded parts divided into groups of 4 gives 6/4 = 3/2 groups.

Worked example

Draw a bar partitioned into 8 equal parts. Shade 7 parts. Circle groups of 1 parts. Use the model to find 7/8 ÷ 1/8. Explain the partial group.

7 Each small part is 1/8; 7 shaded parts divided into groups of 1 gives 7/1 = 7 groups.

  1. Draw a bar partitioned into 6 equal parts. Shade 3 parts. Circle groups of 3 parts. Use the model to find 1/2 ÷ 1/2.
  2. Draw a bar partitioned into 8 equal parts. Shade 3 parts. Circle groups of 3 parts. Use the model to find 3/8 ÷ 3/8.
  3. Draw a bar partitioned into 12 equal parts. Shade 7 parts. Circle groups of 2 parts. Use the model to find 7/12 ÷ 1/6.
  4. Draw a bar partitioned into 10 equal parts. Shade 7 parts. Circle groups of 4 parts. Use the model to find 7/10 ÷ 2/5.
  5. Draw a bar partitioned into 10 equal parts. Shade 4 parts. Circle groups of 3 parts. Use the model to find 2/5 ÷ 3/10.
  6. Draw a bar partitioned into 10 equal parts. Shade 5 parts. Circle groups of 5 parts. Use the model to find 1/2 ÷ 1/2.
  7. Draw a bar partitioned into 10 equal parts. Shade 5 parts. Circle groups of 5 parts. Use the model to find 1/2 ÷ 1/2. Explain the partial group.
  8. Draw a bar partitioned into 10 equal parts. Shade 6 parts. Circle groups of 2 parts. Use the model to find 3/5 ÷ 1/5. Explain the partial group.
  9. Draw a bar partitioned into 8 equal parts. Shade 7 parts. Circle groups of 6 parts. Use the model to find 7/8 ÷ 3/4. Explain the partial group.
  10. Draw a bar partitioned into 12 equal parts. Shade 9 parts. Circle groups of 7 parts. Use the model to find 3/4 ÷ 7/12. Explain the partial group.
Teacher answer key
  1. 1
    Each small part is 1/6; 3 shaded parts divided into groups of 3 gives 3/3 = 1 groups.
    6.NS.A.1
  2. 1
    Each small part is 1/8; 3 shaded parts divided into groups of 3 gives 3/3 = 1 groups.
    6.NS.A.1
  3. 7/2
    Each small part is 1/12; 7 shaded parts divided into groups of 2 gives 7/2 = 7/2 groups.

Day 19 — Unit 2 Fraction Operations and Models

Lesson 4 Partial Groups in Fraction Bars

Standards: 6.NS.A.1

Learning target: I can model fraction division as equal-sized groups.

A quotient asks how many groups of the divisor fit in the dividend. Rename both fractions with a common denominator, draw equal parts, and count groups. A partial group is a fraction of the divisor, not of the original whole.

Worked example

Draw a bar partitioned into 4 equal parts. Shade 2 parts. Circle groups of 1 parts. Use the model to find 1/2 ÷ 1/4.

2 Each small part is 1/4; 2 shaded parts divided into groups of 1 gives 2/1 = 2 groups.

Day 21 — Unit 2 Fraction Operations and Models

Lesson 5 Fraction Division and Reciprocals

Standards: 6.NS.A.1

Learning target: I can divide fractions and check using multiplication.

If (a/b) ÷ (c/d) = q, then (c/d) × q = a/b. Multiplying by d/c undoes multiplying by c/d, so divide by a nonzero fraction by multiplying by its reciprocal. Simplify the product.

Worked example

Calculate 1/2 ÷ 1/4. Simplify.

2 1/2 ÷ 1/4 = 1/2 × 4 = 2.

Worked example

Calculate 1 ÷ 1. Simplify and explain why your answer is reasonable.

1 1 ÷ 1 = 1 × 1 = 1.

  1. Calculate 3/4 ÷ 1/4. Simplify.
  2. Calculate 1/4 ÷ 1/2. Simplify.

  7. Calculate 1 ÷ 1. Simplify and explain why your answer is reasonable.
  8. Calculate 1/6 ÷ 1/2. Simplify and explain why your answer is reasonable.
  9. Calculate 1/5 ÷ 1/2. Simplify and explain why your answer is reasonable.
  10. Calculate 1/5 ÷ 5/2. Simplify and explain why your answer is reasonable.
Teacher answer key

    3/4 ÷ 1/4 = 3/4 × 4 = 3.

  2. 1/2
    1/4 ÷ 1/2 = 1/4 × 2 = 1/2.
    6.NS.A.1
  3. 3/2
    3/4 ÷ 1/2 = 3/4 × 2 = 3/2.
    6.NS.A.1

Lesson 9 Interpreting Fraction Division Stories

Standards: 6.NS.A.1

Learning target: I can write and solve a fraction-division equation for a situation.

Identify the total amount and the size of one group. Use total ÷ group size for the number of groups. A quotient may include a partial group; if the question asks for full servings, use only the whole-number part.

Worked example

A ribbon is 1/4 m long. Each piece is 1/4 m. How many piece-lengths are in the ribbon? Include any fractional piece.

1 piece-lengths 1/4 ÷ 1/4 = 1/4 × 4 = 1.

Worked example

A jug contains 7/8 L. Each full bottle needs 4/3 L. How many full bottles can be filled, and how much liquid remains?

0 bottles; 7/8 L left 7/8 ÷ 4/3 = 7/8 × 3/4 = 21/32. Only whole bottles count; subtract the liquid used.

Day 28 — Unit 2 Fraction Operations and Models

Lesson 11 Missing Factors and Quotients

Standards: 6.NS.A.1

Learning target: I can connect a fraction quotient to a missing factor.

Division and multiplication are inverse operations. For T ÷ s = n, the related equation is s × n = T. Label whether the unknown is the total, the group size, or the number of groups.

Worked example

The equation 1/2 × n = 3/4 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.

n = 3/2; 3/4 ÷ 1/2 = 3/2 3/4 ÷ 1/2 = 3/4 × 2 = 3/2. Check: 1/2 × 3/2 = 3/4.

Worked example

The equation 5/2 × n = 7/3 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.

n = 14/15; 7/3 ÷ 5/2 = 14/15 7/3 ÷ 5/2 = 7/3 × 2/5 = 14/15. Check: 5/2 × 14/15 = 7/3.

  1. The equation 1/4 × n = 1/4 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.
  2. The equation 1/4 × n = 3/4 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.
  3. The equation 1/4 × n = 1/2 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.
  4. The equation 3/2 × n = 3 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.
  5. The equation 3/4 × n = 7/8 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.
  6. The equation 5/2 × n = 8/3 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.
  7. The equation 1/5 × n = 3/5 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.
  8. The equation 1/4 × n = 3/5 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.
  9. The equation 1 × n = 1/8 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.
  10. The equation 1/2 × n = 1/8 represents equally sized portions. Find n, write the related division equation, and check.
Teacher answer key
  1. n = 1; 1/4 ÷ 1/4 = 1
    1/4 ÷ 1/4 = 1/4 × 4 = 1. Check: 1/4 × 1 = 1/4.
    6.NS.A.1
  2. n = 3; 3/4 ÷ 1/4 = 3
    3/4 ÷ 1/4 = 3/4 × 4 = 3. Check: 1/4 × 3 = 3/4.
    6.NS.A.1
  3. n = 2; 1/2 ÷ 1/4 = 2
    1/2 ÷ 1/4 = 1/2 × 4 = 2. Check: 1/4 × 2 = 1/2.
    6.NS.A.1
  4. n = 2; 3 ÷ 3/2 = 2
    3 ÷ 3/2 = 3 × 2/3 = 2. Check: 3/2 × 2 = 3.
    6.NS.A.1
  5. n = 7/6; 7/8 ÷ 3/4 = 7/6
    7/8 ÷ 3/4 = 7/8 × 4/3 = 7/6. Check: 3/4 × 7/6 = 7/8.
    6.NS.A.1
  6. n = 16/15; 8/3 ÷ 5/2 = 16/15
    8/3 ÷ 5/2 = 8/3 × 2/5 = 16/15. Check: 5/2 × 16/15 = 8/3.
    6.NS.A.1
  7. n = 3; 3/5 ÷ 1/5 = 3
    3/5 ÷ 1/5 = 3/5 × 5 = 3. Check: 1/5 × 3 = 3/5.
    6.NS.A.1
  8. n = 12/5; 3/5 ÷ 1/4 = 12/5
    3/5 ÷ 1/4 = 3/5 × 4 = 12/5. Check: 1/4 × 12/5 = 3/5.
    6.NS.A.1
  9. n = 1/8; 1/8 ÷ 1 = 1/8
    1/8 ÷ 1 = 1/8 × 1 = 1/8. Check: 1 × 1/8 = 1/8.
    6.NS.A.1
  10. n = 1/4; 1/8 ÷ 1/2 = 1/4
    1/8 ÷ 1/2 = 1/8 × 2 = 1/4. Check: 1/2 × 1/4 = 1/8.
    6.NS.A.1

Day 29 — Unit 2 Fraction Operations and Models

Lesson 12 Explaining Fraction Division

Standards: 6.NS.A.1

Learning target: I can divide fractions and check using multiplication.

If (a/b) ÷ (c/d) = q, then (c/d) × q = a/b. Multiplying by d/c undoes multiplying by c/d, so divide by a nonzero fraction by multiplying by its reciprocal. Simplify the product.

Worked example

Calculate 1/2 ÷ 1/4. Simplify.

2 1/2 ÷ 1/4 = 1/2 × 4 = 2.

Worked example

Calculate 2 ÷ 3/5. Simplify and explain why your answer is reasonable.

10/3 2 ÷ 3/5 = 2 × 5/3 = 10/3.

  1. Calculate 1/2 ÷ 1/4. Simplify.
  2. Calculate 1 ÷ 1/2. Simplify.
  3. Calculate 1/2 ÷ 1/2. Simplify.
  4. Calculate 7/6 ÷ 1. Simplify.
  5. Calculate 2 ÷ 2. Simplify.
  6. Calculate 3/8 ÷ 3/2. Simplify.
  7. Calculate 2/5 ÷ 1/4. Simplify and explain why your answer is reasonable.
  8. Calculate 4/3 ÷ 1/2. Simplify and explain why your answer is reasonable.
  9. Calculate 1/2 ÷ 4/3. Simplify and explain why your answer is reasonable.
  10. Calculate 2 ÷ 1/5. Simplify and explain why your answer is reasonable.
Teacher answer key
  1. 2
    1/2 ÷ 1/4 = 1/2 × 4 = 2.
    6.NS.A.1
  2. 2
    1 ÷ 1/2 = 1 × 2 = 2.
    6.NS.A.1
  3. 1
    1/2 ÷ 1/2 = 1/2 × 2 = 1.
    6.NS.A.1
  4. 7/6
    7/6 ÷ 1 = 7/6 × 1 = 7/6.
    6.NS.A.1
  5. 1
    2 ÷ 2 = 2 × 1/2 = 1.
    6.NS.A.1
  6. 1/4
    3/8 ÷ 3/2 = 3/8 × 2/3 = 1/4.
    6.NS.A.1
  7. 8/5
    2/5 ÷ 1/4 = 2/5 × 4 = 8/5.
    6.NS.A.1
  8. 8/3
    4/3 ÷ 1/2 = 4/3 × 2 = 8/3.
    6.NS.A.1
  9. 3/8
    1/2 ÷ 4/3 = 1/2 × 3/4 = 3/8.
    6.NS.A.1
  10. 10
    2 ÷ 1/5 = 2 × 5 = 10.
    6.NS.A.1

Day 31 — Unit 3 Ratios and Measurement

Lesson 1 Ratio Language and Order

Standards: 6.RP.A.1

Learning target: I can describe a ratio in the order named.

A ratio compares two quantities multiplicatively. In a:b, a names the first quantity and b names the second. Divide both terms by a common factor to simplify, while keeping the labels and order.

Worked example

A tray has 7 red tiles and 2 blue tiles. Write the red-to-blue ratio in simplest form.

7:2 Keep red first; divide 7:2 by 1.

Worked example

A tray has 40 red tiles and 25 blue tiles. Write the red-to-blue ratio in simplest form and complete: there are ___ times as many red as blue.

8:5; 8/5 times as many Keep red first; divide 40:25 by 5.

Day 32 — Unit 3 Ratios and Measurement

Lesson 2 Parts and the Whole

Standards: 6.RP.A.1

Learning target: I can distinguish part-to-part and part-to-whole comparisons.

For a:b, the total is a+b equal parts. The first part is a/(a+b) of the total; a/b compares it to the second part. Write labels before choosing the denominator.

Worked example

A collection has 25 green and 10 yellow counters. Write the fraction that is green.

5/7 Total = 35; green fraction = 25/35 = 5/7.

Worked example

Day 33 — Unit 3 Ratios and Measurement

Lesson 3 Tape Diagrams and Totals

Standards: 6.RP.A.3a

Learning target: I can use equal-size tape sections to solve a ratio problem.

For a ratio a:b, draw a sections for one quantity and b sections for the other. A total covers a+b sections. Divide the total by a+b to find one section, then multiply by each part count.

Worked example

The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 4:3. There are 35 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count.

20 oaks and 15 pines 7 equal sections total; each section is 35 ÷ 7 = 5.

Worked example

The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 3:2. There are 15 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count and the difference between the counts.

9 oaks and 6 pines; difference 3 5 equal sections total; each section is 15 ÷ 5 = 3.

    6.RP.A.3a
  8. 4; 10
    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 5.
    6.RP.A.3a
  9. 10; 30
    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 6.
    6.RP.A.3a

    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 7.

Day 37 — Unit 3 Ratios and Measurement

Lesson 6 Double Number Lines

Standards: 6.RP.A.3a

Learning target: I can represent a ratio on a double number line.

Place zero on both parallel number lines. Align a known pair above and below one tick. Add equal jumps on each line so aligned values remain in the same ratio.

Worked example

Draw aligned number lines for 6 liters of paint covering 4 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 6 batches. How much area do 36 liters cover?

24 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (6,4), (36,24) 36 ÷ 6 = 6 batches; 6 × 4 = 24.

Worked example

Draw aligned number lines for 5 liters of paint covering 5 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 5 batches. How much area do 25 liters cover?

25 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (5,5), (25,25) 25 ÷ 5 = 5 batches; 5 × 5 = 25.

  1. Draw aligned number lines for 7 liters of paint covering 7 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 3 batches. How much area do 21 liters cover?
  2. Draw aligned number lines for 3 liters of paint covering 3 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 3 batches. How much area do 9 liters cover?
  3. Draw aligned number lines for 2 liters of paint covering 7 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 4 batches. How much area do 8 liters cover?
  4. Draw aligned number lines for 7 liters of paint covering 2 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 3 batches. How much area do 21 liters cover?
  5. Draw aligned number lines for 3 liters of paint covering 6 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 6 batches. How much area do 18 liters cover?
  6. Draw aligned number lines for 6 liters of paint covering 4 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 5 batches. How much area do 30 liters cover?
  7. Draw aligned number lines for 7 liters of paint covering 7 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 4 batches. How much area do 28 liters cover?
  8. Draw aligned number lines for 5 liters of paint covering 7 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 6 batches. How much area do 30 liters cover?
  9. Draw aligned number lines for 3 liters of paint covering 3 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 4 batches. How much area do 12 liters cover?
  10. Draw aligned number lines for 4 liters of paint covering 7 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 7 batches. How much area do 28 liters cover?
Teacher answer key
  1. 21 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (7,7), (21,21)
    21 ÷ 7 = 3 batches; 3 × 7 = 21.
    6.RP.A.3a
  2. 9 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (3,3), (9,9)
    9 ÷ 3 = 3 batches; 3 × 3 = 9.
    6.RP.A.3a
  3. 28 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (2,7), (8,28)
    8 ÷ 2 = 4 batches; 4 × 7 = 28.
    6.RP.A.3a
  4. 6 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (7,2), (21,6)
    21 ÷ 7 = 3 batches; 3 × 2 = 6.
    6.RP.A.3a
  5. 36 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (3,6), (18,36)
    18 ÷ 3 = 6 batches; 6 × 6 = 36.
    6.RP.A.3a
  6. 20 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (6,4), (30,20)
    30 ÷ 6 = 5 batches; 5 × 4 = 20.
    6.RP.A.3a
  7. 28 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (7,7), (28,28)
    28 ÷ 7 = 4 batches; 4 × 7 = 28.
    6.RP.A.3a
  8. 42 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (5,7), (30,42)
    30 ÷ 5 = 6 batches; 6 × 7 = 42.
    6.RP.A.3a
  9. 12 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (3,3), (12,12)
    12 ÷ 3 = 4 batches; 4 × 3 = 12.
    6.RP.A.3a
  10. 49 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (4,7), (28,49)
    28 ÷ 4 = 7 batches; 7 × 7 = 49.
    6.RP.A.3a

Day 38 — Unit 3 Ratios and Measurement

Lesson 7 Comparing Mixtures

Standards: 6.RP.A.3a

Learning target: I can compare ratios using a common quantity.

Scale two ratios until one quantity matches, then compare the other quantity. The larger count alone is not enough: compare the amount per equal-sized batch or per one item.

Worked example

Mix A has 7 cups juice for 5 cups water. Mix B has 39 cups juice for 25 cups water. Which is stronger in juice? Show equivalent ratios.

Mix B For 25 cups water, A uses 35 cups juice; B uses 39. B has more juice per equal water amount.

Worked example

Mix A has 2 cups juice for 4 cups water. Mix B has 13 cups juice for 20 cups water. Which is stronger in juice? Show equivalent ratios.

Lesson 8 Plotting Ratio Pairs

Standards: 6.RP.A.3a

Learning target: I can plot equivalent-ratio pairs and explain a point.

Put the first quantity on the horizontal axis and the second on the vertical axis. Build a table, label units, and plot ordered pairs. A point (x,y) describes both quantities together.

Worked example

(0,0), (8,4), (16,8), (24,12); 24 tickets earns $12 Scale both quantities by each batch count; plot horizontal tickets and vertical dollars.

Day 41 — Unit 3 Ratios and Measurement

Lesson 9 Ratios in Larger Batches

Standards: 6.RP.A.3a

Learning target: I can use equal-size tape sections to solve a ratio problem.

For a ratio a:b, draw a sections for one quantity and b sections for the other. A total covers a+b sections. Divide the total by a+b to find one section, then multiply by each part count.

Worked example

The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 8:6. There are 56 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count.

32 oaks and 24 pines 14 equal sections total; each section is 56 ÷ 14 = 4.

Worked example

The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 6:4. There are 30 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count and the difference between the counts.

18 oaks and 12 pines; difference 6 10 equal sections total; each section is 30 ÷ 10 = 3.

  8. The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 8:6. There are 56 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count and the difference between the counts.

  10. The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 2:7. There are 54 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count and the difference between the counts.

Lesson 10 Customary and Metric Conversions

Standards: 6.RP.A.3d

Learning target: I can convert measurements using equivalent ratios.

Write an equality such as 1 foot = 12 inches. Multiply to change larger units into smaller units; divide for the reverse. A conversion factor is a ratio equal to 1, so the amount stays the same.

Worked example

Convert 2 yards to feet. Show the conversion ratio.

6 feet 2 × 3 = 6. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.

Worked example

Convert 12 feet to yards. Show the conversion ratio.

4 yards 12 ÷ 3 = 4; 1 yards = 3 feet.

  1. Convert 6 hours to minutes. Show the conversion ratio.
  2. Convert 5 liters to milliliters. Show the conversion ratio.
  3. Convert 8 feet to inches. Show the conversion ratio.

  5. Convert 2 liters to milliliters. Show the conversion ratio.
  6. Convert 4000 milliliters to liters. Show the conversion ratio.
  7. Convert 5/2 meters to centimeters. Show the conversion ratio and explain why the numerical value grows.
  8. Convert 15/2 feet to yards. Show the conversion ratio.
  9. Convert 2 hours to minutes. Show the conversion ratio and explain why the numerical value grows.
  10. Convert 42 inches to feet. Show the conversion ratio.
Teacher answer key
  1. 360 minutes
    6 × 60 = 360. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.
    6.RP.A.3d
  2. 5000 milliliters
    5 × 1000 = 5000. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.
    6.RP.A.3d
  3. 96 inches
    8 × 12 = 96. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.
    6.RP.A.3d
  4. 6 meters
    600 ÷ 100 = 6; 1 meters = 100 centimeters.
    6.RP.A.3d
  5. 2000 milliliters
    2 × 1000 = 2000. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.
    6.RP.A.3d
  6. 4 liters
    4000 ÷ 1000 = 4; 1 liters = 1000 milliliters.
    6.RP.A.3d
  7. 250 centimeters
    5/2 × 100 = 250. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.
    6.RP.A.3d
  8. 5/2 yards
    15/2 ÷ 3 = 5/2; 1 yards = 3 feet.
    6.RP.A.3d

    2 × 60 = 120. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.
    6.RP.A.3d

    42 ÷ 12 = 7/2; 1 feet = 12 inches.

Day 45 — Unit 3 Ratios and Measurement

Unit Test Unit 3 Test

Standards: 6.NS.A.1, 6.RP.A.1, 6.RP.A.3a, 6.RP.A.3d

Learning target: I can apply the skills taught so far, show my reasoning, and check my answers.

The first 4 questions review Unit 2.

  1. Draw a bar partitioned into 10 equal parts. Shade 9 parts. Circle groups of 9 parts. Use the model to find 9/10 ÷ 9/10.
  2. Calculate 1/2 ÷ 1/2. Simplify.
  3. A ribbon is 3/4 m long. Each piece is 1/4 m. How many piece-lengths are in the ribbon? Include any fractional piece.
  4. Calculate 2 ÷ 1/8. Simplify.
  5. A tray has 4 red tiles and 5 blue tiles. Write the red-to-blue ratio in simplest form.
  6. A collection has 18 green and 6 yellow counters. Write the fraction that is green.
  7. The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 5:4. There are 45 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count.
  8. A mix uses 5 cups of oats for 6 cups of seeds. Complete the table (oats, seeds): (5, 6), (10, ___), (10, ___).
  9. A mix uses 2 cups of oats for 5 cups of seeds. Complete the table (oats, seeds): (2, 5), (4, ___), (10, ___).
  10. Draw aligned number lines for 3 liters of paint covering 2 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 3 batches. How much area do 9 liters cover?
  11. Mix A has 6 cups juice for 5 cups water. Mix B has 34 cups juice for 25 cups water. Which is stronger in juice? Show equivalent ratios.
  12. For every 5 tickets sold, a club earns $4. Make a table for 0, 1, 2, and 6 batches. Plot (tickets, dollars), label axes, and interpret (30, 24).
  13. The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 6:3. There are 18 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count.
  14. Convert 3/2 yards to feet. Show the conversion ratio and explain why the numerical value grows.
  15. Convert 3/2 meters to centimeters. Show the conversion ratio and explain why the numerical value grows.
  16. Convert 60 minutes to hours. Show the conversion ratio.
  17. A tray has 14 red tiles and 8 blue tiles. Write the red-to-blue ratio in simplest form and complete: there are ___ times as many red as blue.
  18. A collection has 12 green and 24 yellow counters. Write the fraction that is green and the green-to-yellow ratio in simplest form. Explain the different denominators.
  19. The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 6:2. There are 24 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count and the difference between the counts.
  20. A mix uses 2 cups of oats for 4 cups of seeds. Complete the table (oats, seeds): (2, 4), (4, ___), (12, ___) and explain how you know the last two rows are equivalent.
Teacher answer key
  1. 1
    Each small part is 1/10; 9 shaded parts divided into groups of 9 gives 9/9 = 1 groups.
    6.NS.A.1
  2. 1
    1/2 ÷ 1/2 = 1/2 × 2 = 1.
    6.NS.A.1
  3. 3 piece-lengths
    3/4 ÷ 1/4 = 3/4 × 4 = 3.
    6.NS.A.1
  4. 16
    2 ÷ 1/8 = 2 × 8 = 16.
    6.NS.A.1
  5. 4:5
    Keep red first; divide 4:5 by 1.
    6.RP.A.1
  6. 3/4
    Total = 24; green fraction = 18/24 = 3/4.
    6.RP.A.1
  7. 25 oaks and 20 pines
    9 equal sections total; each section is 45 ÷ 9 = 5.
    6.RP.A.3a
  8. 12; 12
    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 2.
    6.RP.A.3a
  9. 10; 25
    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 5.
    6.RP.A.3a
  10. 6 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (3,2), (9,6)
    9 ÷ 3 = 3 batches; 3 × 2 = 6.
    6.RP.A.3a
  11. Mix B
    For 25 cups water, A uses 30 cups juice; B uses 34. B has more juice per equal water amount.
    6.RP.A.3a
  12. (0,0), (5,4), (10,8), (30,24); 30 tickets earns $24
    Scale both quantities by each batch count; plot horizontal tickets and vertical dollars.
    6.RP.A.3a
  13. 12 oaks and 6 pines
    9 equal sections total; each section is 18 ÷ 9 = 2.
    6.RP.A.3a
  14. 9/2 feet
    3/2 × 3 = 9/2. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.
    6.RP.A.3d
  15. 150 centimeters
    3/2 × 100 = 150. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.
    6.RP.A.3d
  16. 1 hours
    60 ÷ 60 = 1; 1 hours = 60 minutes.
    6.RP.A.3d
  17. 7:4; 7/4 times as many
    Keep red first; divide 14:8 by 2.
    6.RP.A.1
  18. 1/3; 1/2 or 1:2; the whole includes both colors
    Total = 36; green fraction = 12/36 = 1/3.
    6.RP.A.1
  19. 18 oaks and 6 pines; difference 12
    8 equal sections total; each section is 24 ÷ 8 = 3.
    6.RP.A.3a
  20. 8; 24
    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 6.
    6.RP.A.3a

Day 46 — Unit 4 Rates and Percents

Lesson 1 Unit Rates with Labels

Standards: 6.RP.A.2, 6.RP.A.3b

Learning target: I can find a unit rate and explain its units.

A unit rate compares an amount with one unit of another quantity. Divide the first quantity by the second. Write the units, such as dollars per notebook, so you know what the quotient means.

Worked example

7 identical notebooks cost $28. Find the cost per notebook.

$4 per notebook Unit rate = 28 ÷ 7 = 4 dollars per notebook.

Worked example

Day 48 — Unit 4 Rates and Percents

Lesson 3 Constant Speed

Standards: 6.RP.A.3b

Learning target: I can solve constant-speed problems using a rate per hour.

At a constant speed, distance = speed × time. Divide distance by time to find speed, or distance by speed to find time. Keep units consistent before calculating.

Worked example

A bus travels at 40 km/h. How long does 200 km take?

5 hours Time = distance ÷ speed = 200 ÷ 40 = 5.

Worked example

A bus travels at 40 km/h. How long does 40 km take?

1 hours Time = distance ÷ speed = 40 ÷ 40 = 1.

  1. A cyclist travels 80 km in 2 hours at constant speed. Find the speed.
  2. A bus travels at 20 km/h. How long does 120 km take?
  3. A cyclist travels 100 km in 5 hours at constant speed. Find the speed.
  4. A bus travels at 10 km/h. How long does 40 km take?
  5. A cyclist travels 245 km in 7 hours at constant speed. Find the speed.
  6. A bus travels at 40 km/h. How long does 200 km take?
  7. A cyclist travels 30 km in 3 hours at constant speed. Find the speed and the distance in 2 hours.
  8. A bus travels at 40 km/h. How long does 60 km take?
  9. A cyclist travels 125/2 km in 5/2 hours at constant speed. Find the speed and the distance in 4 hours.
  10. A bus travels at 35 km/h. How long does 245/2 km take?
Teacher answer key
  1. 40 km/h
    Speed = 80 ÷ 2 = 40; distance = speed × time.
    6.RP.A.3b
  2. 6 hours
    Time = distance ÷ speed = 120 ÷ 20 = 6.
    6.RP.A.3b
  3. 20 km/h
    Speed = 100 ÷ 5 = 20; distance = speed × time.
    6.RP.A.3b
  4. 4 hours
    Time = distance ÷ speed = 40 ÷ 10 = 4.
    6.RP.A.3b
  5. 35 km/h
    Speed = 245 ÷ 7 = 35; distance = speed × time.
    6.RP.A.3b
  6. 5 hours
    Time = distance ÷ speed = 200 ÷ 40 = 5.
    6.RP.A.3b
  7. 10 km/h; 20 km
    Speed = 30 ÷ 3 = 10; distance = speed × time.
    6.RP.A.3b
  8. 3/2 hours
    Time = distance ÷ speed = 60 ÷ 40 = 3/2.
    6.RP.A.3b
  9. 25 km/h; 100 km
    Speed = 125/2 ÷ 5/2 = 25; distance = speed × time.
    6.RP.A.3b
  10. 7/2 hours
    Time = distance ÷ speed = 245/2 ÷ 35 = 7/2.
    6.RP.A.3b

Lesson 7 Percents Fractions and Decimals

Percent means per 100. Write p% as p/100, simplify the fraction, and divide by 100 to write a decimal. To turn a decimal into a percent, multiply by 100.

Day 54 — Unit 4 Rates and Percents

Lesson 8 Finding the Percent Part

Standards: 6.RP.A.3c

Learning target: I can find the part when I know the percent and whole.

Use part = (percent/100) × whole. You may also find 10%, 5%, or 1% and combine equal pieces. Label the part in the same units as the whole.

Worked example

Find 50% of 40.

20 50/100 × 40 = 20.

Worked example

Find 15% of 220. Then explain a mental-math or fraction strategy that checks your answer.

33 15/100 × 220 = 33.

  1. Find 20% of 60.
  2. Find 50% of 60.
  3. Find 25% of 100.
  4. Find 25% of 220.
  5. Find 5% of 180.
  6. Find 15% of 200.
  7. Find 30% of 80. Then explain a mental-math or fraction strategy that checks your answer.
  8. Find 5% of 40. Then explain a mental-math or fraction strategy that checks your answer.
  9. Find 40% of 220. Then explain a mental-math or fraction strategy that checks your answer.

Lesson 10 Finding the Whole

Write part = decimal rate × whole. Divide the part by the decimal rate to find the whole. Check by taking the stated percent of your answer.

Day 58 — Unit 4 Rates and Percents

Lesson 11 Finding the Percent Rate

Standards: 6.RP.A.3c

Learning target: I can find the percent that one quantity is of another.

Divide the part by the whole, then multiply by 100. Keep the same units in part and whole. The percent can be above 100 if the part is larger than the reference whole.

Worked example

40 is what percent of 200?

20% 40 ÷ 200 × 100 = 20%; the reference whole is 200.

Worked example

125% 200 ÷ 160 × 100 = 125%; the reference whole is 160.

Unit Test Unit 4 Test

Standards: 6.RP.A.3a, 6.RP.A.3d, 6.RP.A.2, 6.RP.A.3b, 6.RP.A.3c

  7. A cyclist travels 90 km in 6 hours at constant speed. Find the speed.
  8. A bus travels at 35 km/h. How long does 105 km take?

  10. Pack A has 5 markers for $20; Pack B has 8 markers for $36. Find each unit price and the better buy.

  13. Find 5% of 240.
  14. 60 students are 60% of a school’s enrollment. Find the enrollment.
  15. 16 is what percent of 40? State which quantity is the reference whole.
  16. 44 students are 20% of a school’s enrollment. Find the enrollment.
  17. 5 identical notebooks cost $17.5. Find the cost per notebook and the cost of 8 notebooks at this rate.
  18. 3 identical notebooks cost $12. Find the cost per notebook and the cost of 6 notebooks at this rate.
  19. A cyclist travels 40 km in 1 hours at constant speed. Find the speed and the distance in 5 hours.
  20. A bus travels at 20 km/h. How long does 70 km take?
Teacher answer key
  1. 28 oaks and 16 pines
    11 equal sections total; each section is 44 ÷ 11 = 4.

  2. 10 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (4,5), (8,10)
    8 ÷ 4 = 2 batches; 2 × 5 = 10.
    6.RP.A.3a
  3. 9 oaks and 6 pines
    5 equal sections total; each section is 15 ÷ 5 = 3.
    6.RP.A.3a
  4. 3 yards
    9 ÷ 3 = 3; 1 yards = 3 feet.
    6.RP.A.3d
  5. $4 per notebook
    Unit rate = 16 ÷ 4 = 4 dollars per notebook.
    6.RP.A.2, 6.RP.A.3b
  6. $7 per notebook
    Unit rate = 35 ÷ 5 = 7 dollars per notebook.
    6.RP.A.2, 6.RP.A.3b
  7. 15 km/h
    Speed = 90 ÷ 6 = 15; distance = speed × time.
    6.RP.A.3b

    Time = distance ÷ speed = 105 ÷ 35 = 3.

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 4 with 4.5.

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 4 with 4.5.
    6.RP.A.3b

    30% = 30/100 = 3/10; divide by 100 for 0.3.
    6.RP.A.3c

    75/100 × 220 = 165.
    6.RP.A.3c

    5/100 × 240 = 12.
    6.RP.A.3c

    Whole = 60 ÷ 3/5 = 100. Check 60% × 100 = 60.
    6.RP.A.3c
  15. 40%
    16 ÷ 40 × 100 = 40%; the reference whole is 40.
    6.RP.A.3c
  16. 220
    Whole = 44 ÷ 1/5 = 220. Check 20% × 220 = 44.
    6.RP.A.3c
  17. $3.5 per notebook; $28
    Unit rate = 17.5 ÷ 5 = 3.5 dollars per notebook.
    6.RP.A.2, 6.RP.A.3b
  18. $4 per notebook; $24
    Unit rate = 12 ÷ 3 = 4 dollars per notebook.
    6.RP.A.2, 6.RP.A.3b
  19. 40 km/h; 200 km
    Speed = 40 ÷ 1 = 40; distance = speed × time.

  20. 7/2 hours

Lesson 1 Signed Quantities and Zero

Standards: 6.NS.C.5

Learning target: I can use positive numbers, negative numbers, and zero in context.

Choose a reference point for zero. Opposite directions or values use opposite signs: above sea level is positive and below sea level is negative. Zero means at the chosen reference, not always nothing.

Worked example

-35 m; 0 means at sea level Sea level is the reference. Below is negative and above is positive.

Lesson 2 Opposites and Double Opposites

Standards: 6.NS.C.6a

Learning target: I can identify opposites and the opposite of an opposite.

Opposites are equally far from zero on opposite sides of a number line. The opposite of 0 is 0. Reversing direction twice returns to the original number.

Worked example

7 -7 and 7 are each 7 units from 0 on opposite sides.

Standards: 6.NS.C.6c

-5 < 0 < 2; unit steps Negative -5 is left of or below 0; 2 is right of or above 0. Use equal intervals.

Lesson 5 Comparing Negative Values

Standards: 6.NS.C.7a, 6.NS.C.7b

Learning target: I can order rational numbers using their positions.

A number farther right is greater. Among negative numbers, the value closer to zero is greater. Use a common denominator or familiar benchmarks to compare negative fractions.

Worked example

Compare -8 ___ -4 using <, >, or =. Sketch a number line to justify.

< -8 < -4; greater numbers lie farther right.

Worked example

-1/2 > -7/4; -1/2°C is warmer The greater value is farther right on a number line, even when both are negative.

  6. Compare -5/4 ___ -1 using <, >, or =. Sketch a number line to justify.
  7. Temperatures are -3/10°C and -3/2°C. Write an inequality comparing them and explain which is warmer.
  8. Temperatures are -4/5°C and -1°C. Write an inequality comparing them and explain which is warmer.
  9. Temperatures are -6/5°C and -5/4°C. Write an inequality comparing them and explain which is warmer.
  10. Temperatures are -7/2°C and -7/2°C. Write an inequality comparing them and explain which is warmer.

Day 68 — Unit 5 Rational Numbers and Coordinates

Lesson 7 Absolute Value as Distance

Standards: 6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d

Learning target: I can distinguish a number from its distance to zero.

The absolute value |x| is the distance from x to zero, so it cannot be negative. A larger absolute value means farther from zero, but does not necessarily mean a greater number.

Worked example

Find |-28|. Explain the distance meaning.

28 -28 is 28 units from 0; distance is nonnegative.

Worked example

A diver is at -18 m and a bird is at 3 m relative to sea level. Who is farther from sea level? Who has greater elevation? Use absolute values.

Diver: 18 m from sea level; bird has greater elevation |-18| = 18 > |3| = 3, but -18 < 3.

  1. Find |-16|. Explain the distance meaning.
  2. Find |-35|. Explain the distance meaning.
  3. Find |-6|. Explain the distance meaning.
  4. Find |-3| and compare it with |3/2|.
  5. Find |-6| and compare it with |3|.
  6. Find |-8| and compare it with |4|.
  7. A diver is at -21 m and a bird is at 7/2 m relative to sea level. Who is farther from sea level? Who has greater elevation? Use absolute values.
  8. A diver is at -14 m and a bird is at 2 m relative to sea level. Who is farther from sea level? Who has greater elevation? Use absolute values.
  9. A diver is at -16 m and a bird is at 4 m relative to sea level. Who is farther from sea level? Who has greater elevation? Use absolute values.
  10. A diver is at -25/2 m and a bird is at 5/2 m relative to sea level. Who is farther from sea level? Who has greater elevation? Use absolute values.
Teacher answer key
  1. 16
    -16 is 16 units from 0; distance is nonnegative.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d
  2. 35
    -35 is 35 units from 0; distance is nonnegative.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d
  3. 6
    -6 is 6 units from 0; distance is nonnegative.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d
  4. 3; 3 > 3/2
    -3 is 3 units from 0; distance is nonnegative.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d
  5. 6; 6 > 3
    -6 is 6 units from 0; distance is nonnegative.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d
  6. 8; 8 > 4
    -8 is 8 units from 0; distance is nonnegative.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d
  7. Diver: 21 m from sea level; bird has greater elevation
    |-21| = 21 > |7/2| = 7/2, but -21 < 7/2.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d
  8. Diver: 14 m from sea level; bird has greater elevation
    |-14| = 14 > |2| = 2, but -14 < 2.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d
  9. Diver: 16 m from sea level; bird has greater elevation
    |-16| = 16 > |4| = 4, but -16 < 4.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d

Lesson 9 Points in Four Quadrants

Standards: 6.NS.C.6b, 6.NS.C.6c

Learning target: I can plot ordered pairs in all four quadrants.

Worked example

Quadrant IV Move 4 right, then 6 down.

  10. Plot P(7/2, -3) on a coordinate plane and name its quadrant. Then plot Q on the y-axis at the same height and name its coordinates.

Day 72 — Unit 5 Rational Numbers and Coordinates

Lesson 10 Reflections and Coordinate Signs

Standards: 6.NS.C.6b

Learning target: I can reflect points across an axis using signs.

Across the x-axis, keep x and reverse the sign of y. Across the y-axis, reverse x and keep y. Across both axes, reverse both signs. The reflected point stays the same distance from the axis.

Worked example

Plot (-7, -3), reflect it across y-axis, and give the reflected coordinates.

(7, -3) Reverse the x sign: (7, -3).

Worked example

Plot (-4, -7), reflect it across both axes, and give the reflected coordinates.

(4, 7) Reverse both signs: (4, 7).

  1. Plot (-8, 6), reflect it across x-axis, and give the reflected coordinates.

  5. Plot (-7, 4), reflect it across x-axis, and give the reflected coordinates.
  6. Plot (-2, -7), reflect it across y-axis, and give the reflected coordinates.
  7. Plot (-2, 3), reflect it across both axes, and give the reflected coordinates.
  8. Plot (-7, -3), reflect it across both axes, and give the reflected coordinates.
  9. Plot (-6, 2), reflect it across both axes, and give the reflected coordinates.
  10. Plot (-8, -7), reflect it across both axes, and give the reflected coordinates.
Teacher answer key
  1. (-8, -6)
    Reverse the y sign: (-8, -6).
    6.NS.C.6b
  2. (2, -5)
    Reverse the x sign: (2, -5).
    6.NS.C.6b
  3. (-5, -6)
    Reverse the y sign: (-5, -6).
    6.NS.C.6b
  4. (4, -2)
    Reverse the x sign: (4, -2).
    6.NS.C.6b
  5. (-7, -4)
    Reverse the y sign: (-7, -4).
    6.NS.C.6b
  6. (2, -7)
    Reverse the x sign: (2, -7).
    6.NS.C.6b
  7. (2, -3)
    Reverse both signs: (2, -3).
    6.NS.C.6b
  8. (7, 3)
    Reverse both signs: (7, 3).
    6.NS.C.6b
  9. (6, -2)

Day 73 — Unit 5 Rational Numbers and Coordinates

Lesson 11 Horizontal and Vertical Distances

Standards: 6.NS.C.8

Learning target: I can graph points and find horizontal or vertical distance.

Plot both points. If x-coordinates match, count the vertical units; if y-coordinates match, count horizontal units. On opposite sides of zero, add distances to zero. On the same side, subtract magnitudes.

Worked example

Plot (-6, 2) and (-8, 2). Find their distance.

2 units Only one coordinate changes. Same side of 0: subtract magnitudes to get 2.

Worked example

Plot (-2, 4) and (2, 4). Find their distance. Explain why adding magnitudes works or does not work for these points.

4 units Only one coordinate changes. Opposite sides of 0: 2 + 2 = 4.

  1. Plot (2, -3) and (2, -5). Find their distance.
  2. Plot (-7, 3) and (-10, 3). Find their distance.
  3. Plot (5, -4) and (5, -9). Find their distance.
  4. Plot (-6, 3) and (7, 3). Find their distance.
  5. Plot (5, -2) and (5, 6). Find their distance.
  6. Plot (-3, 2) and (2, 2). Find their distance.
  7. Plot (2, -8) and (2, 2). Find their distance. Explain why adding magnitudes works or does not work for these points.
  8. Plot (-7, 5) and (3, 5). Find their distance. Explain why adding magnitudes works or does not work for these points.
  9. Plot (6, -6) and (6, 4). Find their distance. Explain why adding magnitudes works or does not work for these points.

Day 75 — Unit 5 Rational Numbers and Coordinates

Unit Test Unit 5 Test

Standards: 6.RP.A.3b, 6.RP.A.3c, 6.NS.C.5, 6.NS.C.6a, 6.NS.C.6c, 6.NS.C.7a, 6.NS.C.7b, 6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d, 6.NS.C.6b, 6.NS.C.8

Learning target: I can apply the skills taught so far, show my reasoning, and check my answers.

The first 4 questions review Unit 4.

  1. A cyclist travels 200 km in 5 hours at constant speed. Find the speed.
  2. Pack A has 3 markers for $12; Pack B has 9 markers for $40.5. Find each unit price and the better buy.
  3. Find 50% of 40.
  4. Find 40% of 160.
  5. A marker is 18 m above sea level. Write its signed elevation and explain what elevation 0 means.
  6. Find the opposite of -7. Show both values on a number line.
  7. Draw a horizontal or vertical number line. Plot and label -6, 0, and 6.
  8. Draw a horizontal or vertical number line. Plot and label -1/2, 0, and 3/2.
  9. Compare -1 ___ -1/2 using <, >, or =. Sketch a number line to justify.
  10. Compare -2/5 ___ -5/3 using <, >, or =. Sketch a number line to justify.
  11. Find |-4| and compare it with |2|.
  12. Find |-21| and compare it with |3|.
  13. Plot P(4, 5) on a coordinate plane and name its quadrant.
  14. Plot (-3, 7), reflect it across both axes, and give the reflected coordinates.
  15. Plot (3, -7) and (3, 7). Find their distance. Explain why adding magnitudes works or does not work for these points.
  16. Plot (-6, 3) and (3, 3). Find their distance. Explain why adding magnitudes works or does not work for these points.
  17. A marker is 16 m below sea level. Write its signed elevation and explain what elevation 0 means. Write the elevation of the point equally far on the opposite side of sea level.
  18. Find the opposite of -7/5 and the opposite of that result. Locate all values on a number line.
  19. Draw a horizontal or vertical number line. Plot and label -2/3, 0, and 4/3. Name the step size of your scale and explain the placement.
  20. Draw a horizontal or vertical number line. Plot and label -2, 0, and 7/3. Name the step size of your scale and explain the placement.
Teacher answer key
  1. 40 km/h
    Speed = 200 ÷ 5 = 40; distance = speed × time.
    6.RP.A.3b
  2. A $4/marker; B $4.5/marker; A is cheaper
    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 4 with 4.5.
    6.RP.A.3b
  3. 20
    50/100 × 40 = 20.
    6.RP.A.3c
  4. 64
    40/100 × 160 = 64.
    6.RP.A.3c
  5. +18 m; 0 means at sea level
    Sea level is the reference. Below is negative and above is positive.
    6.NS.C.5
  6. 7
    -7 and 7 are each 7 units from 0 on opposite sides.
    6.NS.C.6a
  7. -6 < 0 < 6; unit steps
    Negative -6 is left of or below 0; 6 is right of or above 0. Use equal intervals.
    6.NS.C.6c
  8. -1/2 < 0 < 3/2; use steps of 1/4
    Negative -1/2 is left of or below 0; 3/2 is right of or above 0. Use equal intervals.
    6.NS.C.6c
  9. <
    -1 < -1/2; greater numbers lie farther right.
    6.NS.C.7a, 6.NS.C.7b
  10. >
    -2/5 > -5/3; greater numbers lie farther right.
    6.NS.C.7a, 6.NS.C.7b
  11. 4; 4 > 2
    -4 is 4 units from 0; distance is nonnegative.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d
  12. 21; 21 > 3
    -21 is 21 units from 0; distance is nonnegative.
    6.NS.C.7c, 6.NS.C.7d
  13. Quadrant I
    Move 4 right, then 5 up.
    6.NS.C.6b, 6.NS.C.6c
  14. (3, -7)
    Reverse both signs: (3, -7).
    6.NS.C.6b
  15. 14 units
    Only one coordinate changes. Opposite sides of 0: 7 + 7 = 14.
    6.NS.C.8
  16. 9 units
    Only one coordinate changes. Opposite sides of 0: 6 + 3 = 9.
    6.NS.C.8
  17. -16 m; 0 means at sea level; opposite +16 m
    Sea level is the reference. Below is negative and above is positive.
    6.NS.C.5
  18. 7/5; opposite again -7/5
    -7/5 and 7/5 are each 7/5 units from 0 on opposite sides.
    6.NS.C.6a
  19. -2/3 < 0 < 4/3; use steps of 1/3
    Negative -2/3 is left of or below 0; 4/3 is right of or above 0. Use equal intervals.
    6.NS.C.6c
  20. -2 < 0 < 7/3; use steps of 1/3
    Negative -2 is left of or below 0; 7/3 is right of or above 0. Use equal intervals.
    6.NS.C.6c

Day 76 — Unit 6 Expressions and Equivalence

Lesson 1 Repeated Factors and Exponents

Standards: 6.EE.A.1

Learning target: I can write repeated multiplication using a whole-number exponent.

The base is the repeated factor and the exponent tells how many copies to multiply. For a positive exponent, 4^3 = 4 × 4 × 4, not 4 × 3. Parentheses show when a whole product is the base.

Worked example

Write 2^4 as repeated multiplication and evaluate.

2 × 2 × 2 × 2 = 16 The exponent counts 4 factors of 2; multiply all factors.

Worked example

Write 7^4 as repeated multiplication and evaluate. Explain why it differs from 7 × 4.

7 × 7 × 7 × 7 = 2401; 7 × 4 = 28 The exponent counts 4 factors of 7; multiply all factors.

  1. Write 3^4 as repeated multiplication and evaluate.
  2. Write 4^4 as repeated multiplication and evaluate.
  3. Write 2^3 as repeated multiplication and evaluate.
  4. Write 6^3 as repeated multiplication and evaluate.
  5. Write 2^4 as repeated multiplication and evaluate.
  6. Write 5^4 as repeated multiplication and evaluate.
  7. Write 7^2 as repeated multiplication and evaluate. Explain why it differs from 7 × 2.
  8. Write 4^3 as repeated multiplication and evaluate. Explain why it differs from 4 × 3.
  9. Write 6^2 as repeated multiplication and evaluate. Explain why it differs from 6 × 2.
  10. Write 2^3 as repeated multiplication and evaluate. Explain why it differs from 2 × 3.
Teacher answer key
  1. 3 × 3 × 3 × 3 = 81
    The exponent counts 4 factors of 3; multiply all factors.

    The exponent counts 4 factors of 4; multiply all factors.
    6.EE.A.1

    The exponent counts 3 factors of 2; multiply all factors.
    6.EE.A.1

    The exponent counts 3 factors of 6; multiply all factors.
    6.EE.A.1

    The exponent counts 4 factors of 2; multiply all factors.
    6.EE.A.1

    The exponent counts 4 factors of 5; multiply all factors.
    6.EE.A.1
  7. 7 × 7 = 49; 7 × 2 = 14
    The exponent counts 2 factors of 7; multiply all factors.
    6.EE.A.1

    The exponent counts 3 factors of 4; multiply all factors.
    6.EE.A.1

Day 81 — Unit 6 Expressions and Equivalence

Lesson 5 Parts of an Expression

Standards: 6.EE.A.2b

Learning target: I can name terms, coefficients, factors, and grouped parts.

Addition and subtraction separate terms. A coefficient multiplies a variable; a constant has no variable. A parenthesized sum can be one factor of a larger product, so describe both the inside and the whole expression.

Worked example

For 5x + 2y + 6, list terms, coefficients, and the constant.

Terms 5x, 2y, 6; coefficients 5, 2; constant 6 Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.

Worked example

In 6(x + 7), identify the outer operation, both factors, and the operation inside the grouped factor.

Outer product; factors 6 and (x + 7); inner sum The complete sum (x + 7) is one factor multiplied by 6.

  1. For 7x + 5y + 3, list terms, coefficients, and the constant.
  2. For 4x + 4y + 6, list terms, coefficients, and the constant.
  3. For (2x + 3) ÷ 2, name the outer operation, the dividend as a single entity, and the divisor. Identify the sum inside the parentheses.
  4. For 3x + 4y + 3, list terms, coefficients, and the constant.
  5. For (2x + 2) ÷ 5, name the outer operation, the dividend as a single entity, and the divisor. Identify the sum inside the parentheses.
  6. For 2x + 6y + 4, list terms, coefficients, and the constant.
  7. For (5x + 6) ÷ 5, name the outer operation, the dividend as a single entity, and the divisor. Identify the sum inside the parentheses.
  8. In 5(x + 3), identify the outer operation, both factors, and the operation inside the grouped factor.
  9. In 3(x + 5), identify the outer operation, both factors, and the operation inside the grouped factor.
  10. In 7(x + 6), identify the outer operation, both factors, and the operation inside the grouped factor.
Teacher answer key
  1. Terms 7x, 5y, 3; coefficients 7, 5; constant 3
    Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.
    6.EE.A.2b
  2. Terms 4x, 4y, 6; coefficients 4, 4; constant 6
    Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.
    6.EE.A.2b
  3. Outer quotient; dividend (2x + 3); divisor 2; inner sum 2x + 3
    The grouped sum is one complete quantity being divided. Within it, the terms are 2x and 3.
    6.EE.A.2b
  4. Terms 3x, 4y, 3; coefficients 3, 4; constant 3
    Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.
    6.EE.A.2b

    The grouped sum is one complete quantity being divided. Within it, the terms are 2x and 2.
    6.EE.A.2b

    Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.
    6.EE.A.2b

    The grouped sum is one complete quantity being divided. Within it, the terms are 5x and 6.

Day 82 — Unit 6 Expressions and Equivalence

Lesson 6 Words to Expressions

Standards: 6.EE.A.2a, 6.EE.B.6

Learning target: I can translate words and situations into expressions.

Define the variable first. Use parentheses for a sum or difference that must stay together. Read subtraction carefully: five less than n means n - 5, while five minus n means 5 - n.

Worked example

Write an expression for 2 less than n.

n - 2 Start with n, then subtract the named amount.

Worked example

A ticket costs $8. A group buys n tickets and pays a single $3 booking fee. Define n and write the total-cost expression. Evaluate it for n = 2.

n = ticket count; 8n + 3; $19 Ticket cost is 8 × n; add the one-time fee 3.

  1. Write an expression for 2 less than n.
  2. Write an expression for 6 less than n.
  3. Write an expression for 5 less than n.
  4. Write an expression for 3 times the sum of x and 2.
  5. Write an expression for 3 times the sum of x and 6.
  6. Write an expression for 3 times the sum of x and 3.
  7. A ticket costs $2. A group buys n tickets and pays a single $7 booking fee. Define n and write the total-cost expression. Evaluate it for n = 3.

Lesson 10 Distributing a Factor

Multiply the factor outside parentheses by every term inside. The two expressions have the same value for every allowed input. An area model can show how one rectangle splits into smaller rectangles.

7x + 28 Multiply the outside factor by every inside term, including the constant.

Day 89 — Unit 6 Expressions and Equivalence

Lesson 12 Proving or Disproving Equivalence

Standards: 6.EE.A.4

Learning target: I can justify whether expressions are equivalent.

Use operation properties to rewrite an expression. Matching values at one input do not prove equivalence for all inputs. A single counterexample is enough to show two expressions are not equivalent.

Worked example

Are 7(x + 3) and 7x + 21 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

Yes; distribution gives 7x + 21 7(x + 3) = 7x + 21. This matches.

Worked example

Are 2(x + 5) and 2x + 10 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

Yes; distribution gives 2x + 10 2(x + 5) = 2x + 10. This matches.

  1. Are 8(x + 5) and 8x + 5 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
  2. Are 4(x + 4) and 4x + 16 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
  3. Are 8(x + 2) and 8x + 2 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
  4. Are 5(x + 3) and 5x + 15 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
  5. Are 7(x + 5) and 7x + 5 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
  6. Are 7(x + 2) and 7x + 14 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
  7. Are 4(x + 3) and 4x + 3 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
  8. Are 6(x + 5) and 6x + 30 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
  9. Are 5(x + 7) and 5x + 7 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
  10. Are 2(x + 4) and 2x + 8 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
Teacher answer key
  1. No; at x = 0, values are 40 and 5
    8(x + 5) = 8x + 40. The constants 40 and 5 differ.
    6.EE.A.4
  2. Yes; distribution gives 4x + 16
    4(x + 4) = 4x + 16. This matches.
    6.EE.A.4
  3. No; at x = 0, values are 16 and 2
    8(x + 2) = 8x + 16. The constants 16 and 2 differ.
    6.EE.A.4
  4. Yes; distribution gives 5x + 15
    5(x + 3) = 5x + 15. This matches.
    6.EE.A.4
  5. No; at x = 0, values are 35 and 5
    7(x + 5) = 7x + 35. The constants 35 and 5 differ.
    6.EE.A.4
  6. Yes; distribution gives 7x + 14
    7(x + 2) = 7x + 14. This matches.
    6.EE.A.4
  7. No; at x = 0, values are 12 and 3
    4(x + 3) = 4x + 12. The constants 12 and 3 differ.

Unit Test Unit 6 Test

Standards: 6.NS.C.6c, 6.NS.C.7a, 6.NS.C.7b, 6.NS.C.6b, 6.NS.C.8, 6.EE.A.1, 6.EE.A.2c, 6.EE.A.2b, 6.EE.A.2a, 6.EE.B.6, 6.EE.A.3, 6.EE.A.4

Teacher answer key
  1. -4 < 0 < 3; unit steps
    Negative -4 is left of or below 0; 3 is right of or above 0. Use equal intervals.
    6.NS.C.6c
  2. <
    -5 < -3; greater numbers lie farther right.
    6.NS.C.7a, 6.NS.C.7b
  3. Quadrant II
    Move 3 left, then 2 up.
    6.NS.C.6b, 6.NS.C.6c
  4. 11 units
    Only one coordinate changes. Opposite sides of 0: 7 + 4 = 11.
    6.NS.C.8
  5. 4 × 4 × 4 × 4 = 256
    The exponent counts 4 factors of 4; multiply all factors.
    6.EE.A.1
  6. 3 × 3 = 9
    The exponent counts 2 factors of 3; multiply all factors.
    6.EE.A.1
  7. 14
    Use parentheses and exponents first, then × and ÷ left to right, then + and -. Result: 14.
    6.EE.A.1, 6.EE.A.2c
  8. 250
    Use parentheses and exponents first, then × and ÷ left to right, then + and -. Result: 250.
    6.EE.A.1, 6.EE.A.2c

    Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.
    6.EE.A.2b

    Group the sum before multiplying.
    6.EE.A.2a, 6.EE.B.6
  11. 2(x + 4)
    Group the sum before multiplying.
    6.EE.A.2a, 6.EE.B.6
  12. 60
    Substitute first: 6^2 + 6 × 4 = 60.
    6.EE.A.2c
  13. 40
    Substitute first: 2^2 + 6 × 6 = 40.
    6.EE.A.2c

    Multiply the outside factor by every inside term, including the constant.

    6.EE.A.3

    3(x + 3) = 3x + 9. This matches.
    6.EE.A.4

Day 91 — Unit 7 Equations Inequalities and Relationships

Lesson 1 Testing Equation Solutions

Standards: 6.EE.B.5

Learning target: I can test a proposed solution by substitution.

Replace the variable with the proposed value, compute each side, and compare. A number is a solution only when it makes the original equation true.

Worked example

Does x = 3 solve 2x + 7 = 13? Show substitution.

Yes; left side = 13 2 × 3 + 7 = 13; compare with 13.

Worked example

Does x = 6 solve 6x + 4 = 40? Show substitution.

Yes; left side = 40 6 × 6 + 4 = 40; compare with 40.

  1. Does x = 3 solve 3x + 4 = 10? Show substitution.
  2. Does x = 4 solve 4x + 4 = 20? Show substitution.
  3. Does x = 6 solve 3x + 6 = 21? Show substitution.
  4. Does x = 6 solve 4x + 4 = 28? Show substitution.
  5. Does x = 3 solve 2x + 7 = 11? Show substitution.
  6. Does x = 4 solve 3x + 3 = 15? Show substitution.
  7. Does x = 4 solve 4x + 7 = 19? Show substitution.
  8. Does x = 3 solve 3x + 2 = 11? Show substitution.
  9. Does x = 5 solve 3x + 2 = 14? Show substitution.
  10. Does x = 5 solve 7x + 2 = 37? Show substitution.
Teacher answer key
  1. No; left side = 13
    3 × 3 + 4 = 13; compare with 10.
    6.EE.B.5
  2. Yes; left side = 20
    4 × 4 + 4 = 20; compare with 20.
    6.EE.B.5
  3. No; left side = 24
    3 × 6 + 6 = 24; compare with 21.
    6.EE.B.5
  4. Yes; left side = 28
    4 × 6 + 4 = 28; compare with 28.
    6.EE.B.5
  5. No; left side = 13
    2 × 3 + 7 = 13; compare with 11.
    6.EE.B.5
  6. Yes; left side = 15
    3 × 4 + 3 = 15; compare with 15.
    6.EE.B.5
  7. No; left side = 23
    4 × 4 + 7 = 23; compare with 19.
    6.EE.B.5

    6.EE.B.5
  9. No; left side = 17

Day 92 — Unit 7 Equations Inequalities and Relationships

Lesson 2 Solving Addition Equations

Standards: 6.EE.B.7

Learning target: I can solve x + p = q and check.

Subtract p from both sides to preserve equality. Then substitute the answer into the original equation. The value of the left side must equal the right side.

Worked example

Solve x + 6 = 9. Check in the original equation.

x = 3 Subtract 6 on both sides. Then x = 3; substitution gives 9 on each side.

Worked example

Solve x + 4 = 6. Check in the original equation.

x = 2 Subtract 4 on both sides. Then x = 2; substitution gives 6 on each side.

  7. Solve x + 3/2 = 3. Check in the original equation.
  8. Solve x + 1 = 5/2. Check in the original equation.
  9. Solve x + 2 = 7/2. Check in the original equation.
  10. Solve x + 2 = 4. Check in the original equation.

Lesson 6 One Step Equation Models

Standards: 6.EE.B.6, 6.EE.B.7

Learning target: I can model a situation with a one-step equation and solve it.

Define the unknown quantity. Translate the relationship into an equation before calculating. Use the inverse operation and check whether the answer makes sense in the situation.

Worked example

A collection grows by 3 cards to 6 cards. Let x be the starting count. Write and solve an equation.

x + 3 = 6; x = 3 cards Subtract 3 from both sides; 3 + 3 = 6.

Worked example
