Day 98 — Unit 7 Equations Inequalities and Relationships

Lesson 7 Writing and Graphing Inequalities

Standards: 6.EE.B.8

Learning target: I can write and graph a constraint using an inequality.

Use > or < for a strict bound and ≥ or ≤ when the endpoint is included. Graph a strict bound with an open circle; use a closed circle for an included endpoint. Shade the direction of all solutions.

Worked example

Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is less than 25. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.

x < 25; open circle at 25, shade left “less than” means <; exclude the endpoint.

Worked example

Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is at least 8. Give one included value and explain whether the endpoint is included.

x ≥ 8; closed circle at 8, shade right “at least” means ≥; include the endpoint. Example solution: 9.

  1. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is more than 16. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.
  2. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is less than 12. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.
  3. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is more than 12. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.
  4. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is less than 24. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.
  5. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is more than 15. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.
  6. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is more than 10. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.
  7. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is at least 49. Give one included value and explain whether the endpoint is included.
  8. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is at least 20. Give one included value and explain whether the endpoint is included.
  9. A museum group must have more than 8 participants. Let p be the participant count. Write an inequality and graph its real-number solution set. State which solutions make sense as participant counts.
  10. A museum group must have at least 42 participants. Let p be the participant count. Write an inequality and graph its real-number solution set. State which solutions make sense as participant counts.
Teacher answer key
  1. x > 16; open circle at 16, shade right
    “more than” means >; exclude the endpoint.
    6.EE.B.8
  2. x < 12; open circle at 12, shade left
    “less than” means <; exclude the endpoint.
    6.EE.B.8
  3. x > 12; open circle at 12, shade right
    “more than” means >; exclude the endpoint.
    6.EE.B.8
  4. x < 24; open circle at 24, shade left
    “less than” means <; exclude the endpoint.
    6.EE.B.8
  5. x > 15; open circle at 15, shade right
    “more than” means >; exclude the endpoint.
    6.EE.B.8
  6. x > 10; open circle at 10, shade right
    “more than” means >; exclude the endpoint.
    6.EE.B.8
  7. x ≥ 49; closed circle at 49, shade right
    “at least” means ≥; include the endpoint. Example solution: 50.
    6.EE.B.8
  8. x ≥ 20; closed circle at 20, shade right
    “at least” means ≥; include the endpoint. Example solution: 21.
    6.EE.B.8
  9. p > 8; open circle at 8, shade right; participant counts are whole numbers 9, 10, …
    “more than” means >. The real-number ray has infinitely many solutions, but participant counts must be whole numbers.
    6.EE.B.8
  10. p ≥ 42; closed circle at 42, shade right; participant counts are whole numbers 42, 43, …
    “at least” means ≥. The real-number ray has infinitely many solutions, but participant counts must be whole numbers.
    6.EE.B.8

Substitute each value and decide whether the comparison is true. A continuous number-line ray represents infinitely many real-number solutions. A real situation may restrict useful solutions to whole numbers.

8, 9 Values below 9 work; the endpoint works too.

    “at least” means ≥. The real-number ray has infinitely many solutions, but participant counts must be whole numbers.
    6.EE.B.8

Day 102 — Unit 7 Equations Inequalities and Relationships

Lesson 10 Dependent and Independent Variables

Standards: 6.EE.C.9

Learning target: I can identify quantities that change together and write a rule.

The independent variable is the input you choose; the dependent variable changes with it. Define both and write the output in terms of the input. A table and graph must show the same rule and units.

Worked example

A shop charges $5 for each of n notebooks. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule.

Independent n = notebooks; dependent C = cost; C = 5n The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.

Worked example

A shop charges $3 for each of n notebooks plus a single $6 delivery fee. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule. What does the cost at n = 0 mean in this model?

Independent n = notebooks; dependent C = cost; C = 3n + 6; C(0) = $6, the fee The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.

Day 105 — Unit 7 Equations Inequalities and Relationships

Unit Test Unit 7 Test

Standards: 6.EE.A.1, 6.EE.A.2c, 6.EE.A.2a, 6.EE.B.6, 6.EE.A.4, 6.EE.B.5, 6.EE.B.7, 6.EE.B.8, 6.EE.C.9

Learning target: I can apply the skills taught so far, show my reasoning, and check my answers.

The first 4 questions review Unit 6.

  1. Evaluate 4 + 2 × 6. Show the intermediate steps.
  2. Write an expression for 5 less than n.
  3. Evaluate 7x + 4 for x = 6.
  4. Are 7(x + 7) and 7x + 49 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.
  5. Does x = 6 solve 8x + 3 = 43? Show substitution.
  6. Solve x + 4 = 7. Check in the original equation.
  7. Solve x - 2 = 1. Check in the original equation.
  8. Solve 2 × x = 5. Check in the original equation.
  9. Solve x ÷ 5 = 3/5. Check in the original equation.
  10. 7 identical lengths of rope total 42 m. Let x be the length of one piece. Write and solve an equation, then check.
  11. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is less than 35. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.
  12. Which values in {7, 8, 9} satisfy x ≤ 8? Test each by substitution.
  13. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is less than 9. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.
  14. A shop charges $3 for each of n notebooks plus a single $6 delivery fee. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule. What does the cost at n = 0 mean in this model?
  15. For y = 5x + 5, make a table for x = 0, 1, 2, 4. Plot the ordered pairs and label both axes. Explain what happens to y each time x increases by 1.
  16. For y = 4x + 5, make a table for x = 0, 1, 2, 3. Plot the ordered pairs and label both axes. Explain what happens to y each time x increases by 1.
  17. Does x = 4 solve 7x + 7 = 35? Show substitution.
  18. Solve x + 3/2 = 5. Check in the original equation.
  19. Solve x - 5/2 = 1/2. Check in the original equation.
  20. Solve 1 × x = 7/2. Check in the original equation.
Teacher answer key
  1. 16
    Use parentheses and exponents first, then × and ÷ left to right, then + and -. Result: 16.
    6.EE.A.1, 6.EE.A.2c
  2. n - 5
    Start with n, then subtract the named amount.
    6.EE.A.2a, 6.EE.B.6
  3. 46
    Substitute first: 7 × 6 + 4 = 46.
    6.EE.A.2c
  4. Yes; distribution gives 7x + 49
    7(x + 7) = 7x + 49. This matches.
    6.EE.A.4
  5. No; left side = 51
    8 × 6 + 3 = 51; compare with 43.
    6.EE.B.5
  6. x = 3
    Subtract 4 on both sides. Then x = 3; substitution gives 7 on each side.
    6.EE.B.7
  7. x = 3
    Add 2 on both sides. Then x = 3; substitution gives 1 on each side.
    6.EE.B.7
  8. x = 5/2
    Divide by 2 on both sides. Then x = 5/2; substitution gives 5 on each side.
    6.EE.B.7
  9. x = 3
    Multiply by 5 on both sides. Then x = 3; substitution gives 3/5 on each side.
    6.EE.B.7
  10. 7x = 42; x = 6 m
    Divide the total by 7: 42 ÷ 7 = 6.
    6.EE.B.6, 6.EE.B.7
  11. x < 35; open circle at 35, shade left
    “less than” means <; exclude the endpoint.
    6.EE.B.8
  12. 7, 8
    Values below 8 work; the endpoint works too.
    6.EE.B.5, 6.EE.B.8
  13. x < 9; open circle at 9, shade left
    “less than” means <; exclude the endpoint.
    6.EE.B.8
  14. Independent n = notebooks; dependent C = cost; C = 3n + 6; C(0) = $6, the fee
    The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.
    6.EE.C.9
  15. (0, 5); (1, 10); (2, 15); (4, 25); y increases by 5
    Substitute each input into y = 5x + 5; x is horizontal and y vertical.
    6.EE.C.9
  16. (0, 5); (1, 9); (2, 13); (3, 17); y increases by 4
    Substitute each input into y = 4x + 5; x is horizontal and y vertical.
    6.EE.C.9
  17. Yes; left side = 35
    7 × 4 + 7 = 35; compare with 35.
    6.EE.B.5
  18. x = 7/2
    Subtract 3/2 on both sides. Then x = 7/2; substitution gives 5 on each side.
    6.EE.B.7
  19. x = 3
    Add 5/2 on both sides. Then x = 3; substitution gives 1/2 on each side.
    6.EE.B.7
  20. x = 7/2
    Divide by 1 on both sides. Then x = 7/2; substitution gives 7/2 on each side.
    6.EE.B.7

Day 106 — Unit 8 Area Volume and Surface Area

Lesson 1 Triangle Areas by Decomposition

Standards: 6.G.A.1

Learning target: I can find the area of any triangle using a perpendicular height.

Two matching triangles form a parallelogram, so A = (base × perpendicular height)/2. A slanted side is not necessarily the height. Label area in square units.

Worked example

Sketch a triangle with base 4 cm and perpendicular height 4 cm. Find its area.

8 cm² A = bh/2 = 4 × 4 ÷ 2 = 8. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

Worked example

Sketch a triangle with base 16 cm and perpendicular height 3 cm. Find its area. Explain why the slanted side is not needed.

24 cm² A = bh/2 = 16 × 3 ÷ 2 = 24. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

Day 108 — Unit 8 Area Volume and Surface Area

Lesson 3 Parallelograms and Trapezoids

Standards: 6.G.A.1

Learning target: I can find parallelogram and trapezoid areas by rearranging shapes.

Cut and move a corner of a parallelogram to make a rectangle: A = bh. Two matching trapezoids form a parallelogram, giving A = (b1 + b2)h/2. Heights must be perpendicular.

Worked example

A parallelogram has base 7 m and perpendicular height 6 m. Sketch how it can be rearranged into a rectangle and find its area.

42 m² Cut and shift a corner; A = bh = 7 × 6 = 42.

Worked example

A trapezoid has parallel bases 14 m and 20 m and perpendicular height 6 m. Sketch it and find its area.

102 m² A = (14 + 20) × 6 ÷ 2 = 102.

  1. A parallelogram has base 3 m and perpendicular height 4 m. Sketch how it can be rearranged into a rectangle and find its area.
  2. A parallelogram has base 7 m and perpendicular height 5 m. Sketch how it can be rearranged into a rectangle and find its area.
  3. A parallelogram has base 2 m and perpendicular height 4 m. Sketch how it can be rearranged into a rectangle and find its area.
  4. A trapezoid has parallel bases 10 m and 22 m and perpendicular height 3 m. Sketch it and find its area.
  5. A trapezoid has parallel bases 10 m and 20 m and perpendicular height 7 m. Sketch it and find its area.
  6. A trapezoid has parallel bases 6 m and 18 m and perpendicular height 3 m. Sketch it and find its area.
  7. A trapezoid has parallel bases 12 m and 22 m and perpendicular height 5 m. Sketch it and find its area.
  8. A trapezoid has parallel bases 4 m and 10 m and perpendicular height 5 m. Sketch it and find its area.
  9. A trapezoid has parallel bases 16 m and 28 m and perpendicular height 6 m. Sketch it and find its area.
  10. A trapezoid has parallel bases 12 m and 24 m and perpendicular height 4 m. Sketch it and find its area.
Teacher answer key
  1. 12 m²
    Cut and shift a corner; A = bh = 3 × 4 = 12.
    6.G.A.1
  2. 35 m²
    Cut and shift a corner; A = bh = 7 × 5 = 35.
    6.G.A.1
  3. 8 m²
    Cut and shift a corner; A = bh = 2 × 4 = 8.
    6.G.A.1
  4. 48 m²
    A = (10 + 22) × 3 ÷ 2 = 48.
    6.G.A.1
  5. 105 m²
    A = (10 + 20) × 7 ÷ 2 = 105.
    6.G.A.1
  6. 36 m²
    A = (6 + 18) × 3 ÷ 2 = 36.
    6.G.A.1
  7. 85 m²
    A = (12 + 22) × 5 ÷ 2 = 85.
    6.G.A.1
  8. 35 m²
    A = (4 + 10) × 5 ÷ 2 = 35.
    6.G.A.1
  9. 132 m²

Lesson 6 Volume with Fractional Edges

Standards: 6.G.A.2

Learning target: I can calculate prism volume with fractional edge lengths.

A rectangular prism has volume V = lwh = Bh, where B is base area. Fractional edge lengths still multiply. Use cubic units; surface area and volume measure different attributes.

Worked example

A rectangular prism has edges 2, 3, and 5 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

30 cm³ B = 2 × 3 = 6 cm²; V = B × 5 = 30 cm³.

Worked example

A rectangular prism has edges 4, 1, and 1 1/2 cm. Sketch it and find its volume. Show both lwh and Bh.

6 cm³ B = 4 × 1 = 4 cm²; V = B × 3/2 = 6 cm³.

Day 114 — Unit 8 Area Volume and Surface Area

Lesson 8 Packing Fractional Unit Cubes

Standards: 6.G.A.2

Learning target: I can connect fractional unit cubes with a prism volume formula.

For cubes with edge 1/n, each cube has volume 1/n^3 cubic unit. Divide each prism edge by 1/n to count cubes along it. Multiply the counts, then multiply by the volume of one small cube.

Worked example

Fill a prism 5/2 × 2 × 1 units with cubes of edge 1/2 unit. How many cubes fit? Use their volumes to verify lwh.

40 cubes; each 1/8 cubic unit; prism 5 cubic units Counts along edges: 5, 4, 2. Product 40; 40 × 1/8 = 5, also lwh.

Worked example

Fill a prism 5/3 × 7/3 × 5/3 units with cubes of edge 1/3 unit. How many cubes fit? Use their volumes to verify lwh.

175 cubes; each 1/27 cubic unit; prism 175/27 cubic units Counts along edges: 5, 7, 5. Product 175; 175 × 1/27 = 175/27, also lwh.

Lesson 9 Drawing and Labeling Nets

Standards: 6.G.A.4

Learning target: I can draw a net and account for every face of a solid.

A net unfolds all faces into a plane without overlap. Rectangular prisms have three matching pairs of rectangles. Pyramids have a polygonal base and triangular sides. Add face areas, not edge lengths.

Worked example

Draw a net for a closed 3 × 4 × 5 cm rectangular prism. Label the six faces and calculate total area.

2 faces each of 3×4, 3×5, 4×5; 94 cm² Add 2(12) + 2(15) + 2(20) = 94.

Worked example

Draw a net for a triangular prism. Its two congruent right-triangle bases have legs 3 cm and 4 cm and hypotenuse 5 cm; prism length is 5 cm. Label all faces and find surface area.

2 triangles (area 6 each), rectangles 3×5, 4×5, 5×5; SA 72 cm² Triangle pair: 12 cm²; rectangles: (3+4+5) × 5 = 60; total 72.

  8. Draw a net for a triangular prism. Its two congruent right-triangle bases have legs 3 cm and 4 cm and hypotenuse 5 cm; prism length is 4 cm. Label all faces and find surface area.

Day 117 — Unit 8 Area Volume and Surface Area

Lesson 10 Prism Surface Area

Standards: 6.G.A.4

Learning target: I can use a rectangular-prism net to find surface area.

A closed rectangular prism has two faces of area lw, two of lh, and two of wh. Add the six face areas: SA = 2lw + 2lh + 2wh. If a face is missing, do not include it.

Worked example

A 6 cm by 2 cm base and 2 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.

56 cm² Closed surface area = 2(12+12+4) = 56.

Worked example

A 7 cm by 5 cm base and 4 cm height form an open-top box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.

131 cm² Closed surface area = 2(35+28+20) = 166. Remove the top area 35: 131.

  1. A 8 cm by 4 cm base and 5 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  2. A 7 cm by 5 cm base and 6 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  3. A 2 cm by 5 cm base and 6 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  4. A 4 cm by 7 cm base and 4 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  5. A 8 cm by 4 cm base and 4 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  6. A 2 cm by 2 cm base and 2 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  7. A 4 cm by 6 cm base and 4 cm height form an open-top box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.

Lesson 11 Pyramid Nets and Surface Area

Standards: 6.G.A.4

Learning target: I can find pyramid surface area from its triangular faces and base.

For a square pyramid, draw one square and four triangles. Each triangle has area base edge × face height / 2. The face height is measured on the triangular face, not vertically inside the solid.

Worked example

A square pyramid has base edge 16 cm. Each triangular face has perpendicular face height 10 cm. Draw a net and calculate total surface area.

576 cm² Base = 256; each triangle = 16 × 10 ÷ 2 = 80; add four triangles to get 576.

Worked example

Day 119 — Unit 8 Area Volume and Surface Area

Lesson 12 Choosing Area Volume or Surface Area

Standards: 6.G.A.1, 6.G.A.2, 6.G.A.4

Learning target: I can choose the correct geometric measure and justify the units.

Read the requested quantity first and identify the relevant model. Two matching triangles form a parallelogram, so A = (base × perpendicular height)/2. A slanted side is not necessarily the height. Label area in square units. Explain why the units and the final value fit the situation.

Worked example

A rectangular prism has edges 3, 1 1/2, and 6 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

27 cm³ B = 3 × 3/2 = 9/2 cm²; V = B × 6 = 27 cm³.

Worked example

A 3 cm by 5 cm base and 4 cm height form an open-top box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.

79 cm² Closed surface area = 2(15+12+20) = 94. Remove the top area 15: 79.

  1. Sketch a triangle with base 16 cm and perpendicular height 3 cm. Find its area.
  2. A rectangular prism has edges 6, 1, and 5 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.
  3. A 6 cm by 2 cm base and 2 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  4. An L-shaped garden is a 11 m by 11 m rectangle with an 7 m by 7 m corner rectangle removed. Sketch and find the garden area.
  5. A square pyramid has base edge 8 cm. Each triangular face has perpendicular face height 7 cm. Draw a net and calculate total surface area.
  6. Sketch a triangle with base 8 cm and perpendicular height 2 cm. Find its area.
  7. A rectangular prism has edges 2 1/2, 2 1/2, and 3 cm. Sketch it and find its volume. Show both lwh and Bh.
  8. A 4 cm by 7 cm base and 3 cm height form an open-top box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  9. A 11 m by 7 m rectangle has a right-triangle corner removed with perpendicular legs 3 m and 4 m. Sketch the remaining polygon and find its area.
  10. A square pyramid has base edge 14 cm. Each triangular face has perpendicular face height 10 cm. Draw a net and calculate total surface area. Explain why the vertical pyramid height is not needed.
Teacher answer key
  1. 24 cm²
    A = bh/2 = 16 × 3 ÷ 2 = 24. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.
    6.G.A.1
  2. 30 cm³
    B = 6 × 1 = 6 cm²; V = B × 5 = 30 cm³.
    6.G.A.2
  3. 56 cm²
    Closed surface area = 2(12+12+4) = 56.
    6.G.A.4
  4. 72 m²
    Outer area 121 minus cutout 49 = 72.
    6.G.A.1
  5. 176 cm²
    Base = 64; each triangle = 8 × 7 ÷ 2 = 28; add four triangles to get 176.
    6.G.A.4
  6. 8 cm²
    A = bh/2 = 8 × 2 ÷ 2 = 8. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.
    6.G.A.1
  7. 75/4 cm³
    B = 5/2 × 5/2 = 25/4 cm²; V = B × 3 = 75/4 cm³.
    6.G.A.2
  8. 94 cm²
    Closed surface area = 2(28+12+21) = 122. Remove the top area 28: 94.
    6.G.A.4
  9. 71 m²
    Whole rectangle 77; remove triangle 6; difference 71.
    6.G.A.1
  10. 476 cm²
    Base = 196; each triangle = 14 × 10 ÷ 2 = 70; add four triangles to get 476.
    6.G.A.4

Unit Test Unit 8 Test

Standards: 6.EE.B.7, 6.EE.B.6, 6.EE.B.8, 6.EE.C.9, 6.G.A.1, 6.G.A.3, 6.G.A.2, 6.G.A.4

The first 4 questions review Unit 7.

  1. Solve x - 7 = 5/2. Check in the original equation.
  2. A collection grows by 8 cards to 32 cards. Let x be the starting count. Write and solve an equation.
  3. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is less than 16. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.
  4. For y = 6x + 4, make a table for x = 0, 1, 2, 6. Plot the ordered pairs and label both axes.
  5. Sketch a triangle with base 10 cm and perpendicular height 6 cm. Find its area.
  6. Sketch a triangle with base 10 cm and perpendicular height 2 cm. Find its area.

  8. An L-shaped garden is a 9 m by 8 m rectangle with an 7 m by 6 m corner rectangle removed. Sketch and find the garden area.

  16. A 4 cm by 7 cm base and 4 cm height form an open-top box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  17. Sketch a triangle with base 14 cm and perpendicular height 2 cm. Find its area. Explain why the slanted side is not needed.
  18. Sketch a triangle with base 6 cm and perpendicular height 7/2 cm. Find its area. Explain why the slanted side is not needed.
  19. A trapezoid has parallel bases 8 m and 20 m and perpendicular height 4 m. Sketch it and find its area.
  20. A 9 m by 10 m rectangle has a right-triangle corner removed with perpendicular legs 5 m and 5 m. Sketch the remaining polygon and find its area.
Teacher answer key
  1. x = 19/2
    Add 7 on both sides. Then x = 19/2; substitution gives 5/2 on each side.
    6.EE.B.7
  2. x + 8 = 32; x = 24 cards
    Subtract 8 from both sides; 24 + 8 = 32.
    6.EE.B.6, 6.EE.B.7
  3. x < 16; open circle at 16, shade left
    “less than” means <; exclude the endpoint.
    6.EE.B.8
  4. (0, 4); (1, 10); (2, 16); (6, 40)
    Substitute each input into y = 6x + 4; x is horizontal and y vertical.
    6.EE.C.9
  5. 30 cm²
    A = bh/2 = 10 × 6 ÷ 2 = 30. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.
    6.G.A.1
  6. 10 cm²
    A = bh/2 = 10 × 2 ÷ 2 = 10. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.
    6.G.A.1
  7. 9 m²
    Cut and shift a corner; A = bh = 3 × 3 = 9.
    6.G.A.1
  8. 30 m²
    Outer area 72 minus cutout 42 = 30.
    6.G.A.1
  9. Sides 10 and 8; area 80 square units
    Horizontal length 2 - (-8) = 10; vertical length 2 - (-6) = 8; area = 10 × 8.
    6.G.A.3, 6.G.A.1
  10. 40 cm³
    B = 4 × 2 = 8 cm²; V = B × 5 = 40 cm³.
    6.G.A.2
  11. 20 cm³
    B = 4 × 5/2 = 10 cm²; V = B × 2 = 20 cm³.
    6.G.A.2
  12. 48 cubes; each 1/8 cubic unit; prism 6 cubic units
    Counts along edges: 4, 4, 3. Product 48; 48 × 1/8 = 6, also lwh.
    6.G.A.2
  13. 2 faces each of 2×3, 2×4, 3×4; 52 cm²
    Add 2(6) + 2(8) + 2(12) = 52.
    6.G.A.4
  14. 80 cm²
    Closed surface area = 2(10+25+10) = 90. Remove the top area 10: 80.
    6.G.A.4
  15. 432 cm²
    Base = 144; each triangle = 12 × 12 ÷ 2 = 72; add four triangles to get 432.
    6.G.A.4
  16. 116 cm²
    Closed surface area = 2(28+16+28) = 144. Remove the top area 28: 116.
    6.G.A.4
  17. 14 cm²
    A = bh/2 = 14 × 2 ÷ 2 = 14. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.
    6.G.A.1
  18. 21/2 cm²
    A = bh/2 = 6 × 7/2 ÷ 2 = 21/2. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.
    6.G.A.1
  19. 56 m²
    A = (8 + 20) × 4 ÷ 2 = 56.
    6.G.A.1
  20. 155/2 m²
    Whole rectangle 90; remove triangle 25/2; difference 155/2.
    6.G.A.1

Day 121 — Unit 9 Statistics and Data Displays

Lesson 1 Questions That Anticipate Variability

Standards: 6.SP.A.1

Learning target: I can recognize a statistical question and the expected variability.

A statistical question anticipates different answers in a collection of data. Name the group, the measured attribute, and the expected variation. A question about one fixed fact is not statistical.

Worked example

Is “How many centimeters are in one meter?” statistical? Explain.

No; one fixed conversion, 100 cm. Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

Worked example

A survey asks “Is our school nice?” Improve it to collect numerical data with expected variation.

Example: On a 1 to 5 scale, how do students in our class rate the school library? Ratings vary among students; the numerical scale must be defined. Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

  1. Is “How many pages did each student read last night?” statistical? Explain.
  2. Is “How many centimeters are in one meter?” statistical? Explain.
  3. Is “What are the heights of the plants in our garden?” statistical? Explain.
  4. Identify the group, attribute, and units in: How many minutes do students in our class sleep each night?
  5. Rewrite “How long is my pencil?” as a statistical question.
  6. A student asks only one person’s age once. Explain why “How old is this person today?” is not statistical.
  7. Write a statistical question about travel to school. Name the group, attribute, units, and why answers may vary.
  8. A survey asks “Is our school nice?” Improve it to collect numerical data with expected variation.
  9. Plan a class investigation of daily water use. Write a statistical question, identify a unit, and describe how to collect comparable data.
  10. Critique: “A question is statistical whenever it contains numbers.” Give a counterexample and a corrected rule.
Teacher answer key
  1. Yes; pages read vary across students.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  2. No; one fixed conversion, 100 cm.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  3. Yes; plant heights vary.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  4. Group: class; attribute: sleep duration; units: minutes.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  5. Example: What are the lengths in centimeters of the pencils in our classroom?
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  6. It seeks one fixed value, rather than anticipating variation in a data collection.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  7. Example: How many minutes do students in our class take to reach school? Group: class; travel time in minutes varies with routes and travel methods.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  8. Example: On a 1 to 5 scale, how do students in our class rate the school library? Ratings vary among students; the numerical scale must be defined.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  9. Example: How many liters of water do students in our class drink in one day? Record each student’s total over the same 24-hour period in liters; amounts can differ.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  10. Counterexample: How many sides does a triangle have? It has a fixed answer. A statistical question anticipates variability in a collection of data.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1

Day 122 — Unit 9 Statistics and Data Displays

Lesson 2 Center Spread and Shape

Standards: 6.SP.A.2, 6.SP.A.3

Learning target: I can describe a distribution by center, spread, and shape.

A distribution shows which values occur and how often. Describe a typical value, how spread out the values are, and whether the shape is roughly symmetric, skewed, clustered, or has unusual values.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 7, 8, 8, 9, 9, 10. Describe a typical value, range, shape, and any striking value.

Center near 17/2 min; range 3 min; roughly symmetric; no isolated extreme The values balance around the middle. Range = 10 - 7.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Describe a typical value, range, shape, and any striking value.

Center near 7/2 min; range 12 min; right-skewed with high value 14 min The values cluster low with a long high tail. Range = 14 - 2.

Day 123 — Unit 9 Statistics and Data Displays

Lesson 3 Mean and Observation Counts

Standards: 6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c

Learning target: I can calculate and interpret the mean of numerical data.

The mean is a fair share: add all values and divide by the number of observations. Report the count and measurement units. A large unusual value can pull the mean toward it.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 7, 8. Report n, units, and mean.

n = 6; minutes; mean 13/2 min Sum = 39; mean = 39 ÷ 6 = 13/2.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 8, 17. Report n, units, and mean. If one more student reads 9 minutes, find the new mean.

n = 6; minutes; mean 49/6 min; new mean 58/7 min Sum = 49; mean = 49 ÷ 6 = 49/6. New sum = 58; divide by 7.

Day 124 — Unit 9 Statistics and Data Displays

Lesson 4 Medians and Unusual Values

Standards: 6.SP.B.5c

Learning target: I can find a median and compare it with the mean.

Order the data first. For an odd count, choose the middle value. For an even count, average the two middle values. The median often resists an unusually high or low value better than the mean.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 4, 5, 5, 6, 6, 7. Find the median. Show the middle positions.

Median 11/2 min 6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (5 + 6) ÷ 2 = 11/2.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 8, 9, 9, 10, 11, 20. Find the median and mean. Explain the difference in this set.

Median 19/2 min; mean 67/6 min; the high value pulls the mean upward 6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (9 + 10) ÷ 2 = 19/2.

  1. Reading times in minutes are 4, 5, 5, 6, 6, 7. Find the median. Show the middle positions.
  2. Reading times in minutes are 3, 4, 4, 5, 5, 6. Find the median. Show the middle positions.
  3. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 5. Find the median. Show the middle positions.
  4. Reading times in minutes are 8, 10, 10, 12, 12, 14. Find the median. Show the middle positions.
  5. Reading times in minutes are 4, 8, 8, 12, 12, 16. Find the median. Show the middle positions.
  6. Reading times in minutes are 2, 7, 7, 12, 12, 17. Find the median. Show the middle positions.
  7. Reading times in minutes are 6, 7, 7, 8, 9, 18. Find the median and mean. Explain the difference in this set.
  8. Reading times in minutes are 4, 5, 5, 6, 7, 16. Find the median and mean. Explain the difference in this set.
  9. Reading times in minutes are 3, 4, 4, 5, 6, 15. Find the median and mean. Explain the difference in this set.
  10. Reading times in minutes are 8, 9, 9, 10, 11, 20. Find the median and mean. Explain the difference in this set.
Teacher answer key
  1. Median 11/2 min
    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (5 + 6) ÷ 2 = 11/2.
    6.SP.B.5c
  2. Median 9/2 min
    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (4 + 5) ÷ 2 = 9/2.

    6.SP.B.5c

    6.SP.B.5c
  6. Median 19/2 min
    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (7 + 12) ÷ 2 = 19/2.
    6.SP.B.5c
  7. Median 15/2 min; mean 55/6 min; the high value pulls the mean upward
    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (7 + 8) ÷ 2 = 15/2.
    6.SP.B.5c

Day 126 — Unit 9 Statistics and Data Displays

Lesson 5 Quartiles and Interquartile Range

Standards: 6.SP.A.3, 6.SP.B.5c

Learning target: I can calculate quartiles and interquartile range.

Sort the data. Find the median, then the median of each half for Q1 and Q3. For an odd count, exclude the overall median from both halves in this course. IQR = Q3 - Q1; range = maximum - minimum.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 7, 8, 8, 9, 9, 10. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.

Q1 8, Q3 9; IQR 1 min; range 3 min Lower half [7, 8, 8] has median 8; upper half [9, 9, 10] has median 9. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 3, 4, 4, 5, 6, 15. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range. Explain what IQR describes.

Q1 4, Q3 6; IQR 2 min; range 12 min; IQR is the span of the middle half Lower half [3, 4, 4] has median 4; upper half [5, 6, 15] has median 6. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

  1. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 5. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.
  2. Reading times in minutes are 6, 7, 7, 8, 8, 9. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.
  3. Reading times in minutes are 8, 9, 9, 10, 10, 11. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.
  4. Reading times in minutes are 3, 8, 8, 13, 13, 18. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.
  5. Reading times in minutes are 5, 8, 8, 11, 11, 14. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.
  6. Reading times in minutes are 5, 7, 7, 9, 9, 11. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.
  7. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range. Explain what IQR describes.
  8. Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 8, 17. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range. Explain what IQR describes.
  9. Reading times in minutes are 7, 8, 8, 9, 10, 19. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range. Explain what IQR describes.
  10. Reading times in minutes are 8, 9, 9, 10, 11, 20. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range. Explain what IQR describes.

Day 127 — Unit 9 Statistics and Data Displays

Lesson 6 Mean Absolute Deviation

Standards: 6.SP.B.5c

Learning target: I can calculate and interpret mean absolute deviation.

Find the mean. Calculate each value’s distance from the mean, add those distances, then divide by the number of observations. MAD describes a typical distance from the mean in the original units.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 7, 8. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.

Mean 13/2 min; MAD 5/6 min Distances: 3/2, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2, 3/2; sum 5; divide by 6 = 5/6.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 8, 17. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations. Interpret MAD in a sentence.

Mean 49/6 min; MAD 53/18 min; a typical distance from the mean is 53/18 min Distances: 19/6, 13/6, 13/6, 7/6, 1/6, 53/6; sum 53/3; divide by 6 = 53/18.

  1. Reading times in minutes are 8, 9, 9, 10, 10, 11. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.
  2. Reading times in minutes are 7, 8, 8, 9, 9, 10. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.
  3. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 5. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.
  4. Reading times in minutes are 8, 10, 10, 12, 12, 14. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.
  5. Reading times in minutes are 8, 12, 12, 16, 16, 20. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.
  6. Reading times in minutes are 4, 6, 6, 8, 8, 10. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.
  7. Reading times in minutes are 4, 5, 5, 6, 7, 16. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations. Interpret MAD in a sentence.
  8. Reading times in minutes are 3, 4, 4, 5, 6, 15. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations. Interpret MAD in a sentence.
  9. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations. Interpret MAD in a sentence.
  10. Reading times in minutes are 7, 8, 8, 9, 10, 19. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations. Interpret MAD in a sentence.

    Distances: 19/6, 13/6, 13/6, 7/6, 1/6, 53/6; sum 53/3; divide by 6 = 53/18.
    6.SP.B.5c

Lesson 7 Choosing Measures of Center

Standards: 6.SP.B.5d

Learning target: I can choose a measure of center and explain why.

Use the shape and context. Mean with mean absolute deviation can describe fairly symmetric data. Median with interquartile range often describes skewed data or data with an unusual value more fairly.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 5. Choose a center and matching variability measure. Calculate both and justify your choice.

Mean 7/2 min and MAD 5/6 min; roughly symmetric data Mean = 7/2, median = 7/2, IQR = 4 - 3 = 1, MAD = 5/6. Accept another justified choice tied to shape and purpose.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 7, 8, 8, 9, 10, 19. Choose a center and matching variability measure. Calculate both and justify your choice.

Median 17/2 min and IQR 2 min; high value makes these resistant measures useful Mean = 61/6, median = 17/2, IQR = 10 - 8 = 2, MAD = 53/18. Accept another justified choice tied to shape and purpose.

  1. Reading times in minutes are 4, 5, 5, 6, 6, 7. Choose a center and matching variability measure. Calculate both and justify your choice.

Lesson 8 Dot Plots on a Number Line

Standards: 6.SP.B.4

Learning target: I can construct and interpret a dot plot on a number line.

Draw an equally spaced number line and stack one dot per observation above its value. Repeated values stack vertically. Add an axis label with units and check that the dot count equals the data count.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 7, 8. Draw a labeled dot plot. Check the number of dots.

5: 1 dot; 6: 2 dots; 7: 2 dots; 8: 1 dot; n = 6 Each observation contributes one dot; stack repeated values at their number-line position.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Draw a labeled dot plot. Describe a cluster or gap and report the count.

2: 1 dot; 3: 2 dots; 4: 1 dot; 5: 1 dot; 14: 1 dot; n = 6; low cluster and high isolated value Each observation contributes one dot; stack repeated values at their number-line position.

Day 131 — Unit 9 Statistics and Data Displays

Lesson 9 Histograms and Intervals

Standards: 6.SP.B.4

Learning target: I can construct and interpret a histogram with equal-width bins.

Group numerical values into adjacent, nonoverlapping intervals. Count each observation in exactly one interval, draw touching bars, and label the numerical axis and the frequency axis.

Worked example

Daily reading times in minutes: 3, 16, 11, 3, 1, 19. Draw a histogram using bins 0–4, 5–9, 10–14, and 15–19. Label axes.

Frequencies 3, 0, 1, 2 Count every value once. Bars touch and have equal width; horizontal axis is minutes, vertical axis is frequency.

Worked example

Daily reading times in minutes: 2, 5, 6, 19, 18, 14, 17, 3, 3, 14. Draw a histogram using bins 0–4, 5–9, 10–14, and 15–19. Label axes. Which interval has the greatest frequency? Include ties.

Frequencies 3, 2, 2, 3; greatest: 0–4, 15–19 Count every value once. Bars touch and have equal width; horizontal axis is minutes, vertical axis is frequency.

  1. Daily reading times in minutes: 11, 7, 14, 11, 7, 8. Draw a histogram using bins 0–4, 5–9, 10–14, and 15–19. Label axes.
  2. Daily reading times in minutes: 14, 13, 7, 11, 9, 3. Draw a histogram using bins 0–4, 5–9, 10–14, and 15–19. Label axes.
  3. Daily reading times in minutes: 10, 9, 2, 15, 15, 6. Draw a histogram using bins 0–4, 5–9, 10–14, and 15–19. Label axes.
  4. Daily reading times in minutes: 13, 4, 3, 10, 1, 15, 15, 16, 18, 5. Draw a histogram using bins 0–4, 5–9, 10–14, and 15–19. Label axes.
  5. Daily reading times in minutes: 10, 10, 10, 0, 4, 19, 0, 0, 4, 17. Draw a histogram using bins 0–4, 5–9, 10–14, and 15–19. Label axes.

Lesson 11 Comparing Data Sets

Standards: 6.SP.A.2, 6.SP.B.5d

Learning target: I can compare centers and variability in two data sets.

Compute comparable measures for both groups. Compare typical values and consistency using the same units. State what the data support without claiming that every member of one group exceeds every member of the other.

Worked example

Reading times (min): Group A [2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 5], Group B [4, 5, 5, 6, 6, 7]. Compare median and IQR. Which group reads longer typically? Which is more consistent?

A median 7/2, B median 11/2 min; both IQR 1 min. B is typically 2 min higher; equal middle-half variability. Adding 2 shifts all values and the median by 2, but does not change Q3 - Q1.

Day 135 — Unit 9 Statistics and Data Displays

Unit Test Unit 9 Test

Standards: 6.G.A.1, 6.G.A.2, 6.G.A.4, 6.SP.A.1, 6.SP.A.2, 6.SP.A.3, 6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c, 6.SP.B.5d, 6.SP.B.4

Learning target: I can apply the skills taught so far, show my reasoning, and check my answers.

The first 4 questions review Unit 8.

  1. A parallelogram has base 5 m and perpendicular height 7 m. Sketch how it can be rearranged into a rectangle and find its area.
  2. A rectangular prism has edges 2, 3 1/2, and 4 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.
  3. Draw a net for a closed 2 × 3 × 6 cm rectangular prism. Label the six faces and calculate total area.
  4. An L-shaped garden is a 10 m by 8 m rectangle with an 6 m by 4 m corner rectangle removed. Sketch and find the garden area.
  5. Is “What are the heights of the plants in our garden?” statistical? Explain.
  6. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 5. Describe a typical value, range, shape, and any striking value.
  7. Reading times in minutes are 8, 9, 9, 10, 10, 11. Report n, units, and mean.
  8. Reading times in minutes are 8, 12, 12, 16, 16, 20. Find the median. Show the middle positions.
  9. Reading times in minutes are 8, 11, 11, 14, 14, 17. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.
  10. Reading times in minutes are 6, 10, 10, 14, 14, 18. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.
  11. Reading times in minutes are 4, 10, 10, 16, 16, 22. Choose a center and matching variability measure. Calculate both and justify your choice.
  12. Reading times in minutes are 7, 13, 13, 19, 19, 25. Draw a labeled dot plot. Check the number of dots.
  13. Daily reading times in minutes: 9, 12, 17, 10, 17, 3, 7, 1, 13, 2. Draw a histogram using bins 0–4, 5–9, 10–14, and 15–19. Label axes.
  14. Reading times in minutes are 6, 7, 7, 8, 9, 18. Find the five-number summary and draw a labeled box plot. State the IQR.
  15. Reading times (min): Group A [8, 9, 9, 10, 11, 20], Group B [10, 11, 11, 12, 13, 22]. Compare median and IQR. Which group reads longer typically? Which is more consistent?
  16. Reading times in minutes are 3, 4, 4, 5, 6, 15. Write a summary with count, attribute, units, center, variability, shape, and a justified choice of measures.
  17. A survey asks “Is our school nice?” Improve it to collect numerical data with expected variation.
  18. Reading times in minutes are 3, 4, 4, 5, 6, 15. Describe a typical value, range, shape, and any striking value.
  19. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Report n, units, and mean. If one more student reads 6 minutes, find the new mean.
  20. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Find the median and mean. Explain the difference in this set.
Teacher answer key
  1. 35 m²
    Cut and shift a corner; A = bh = 5 × 7 = 35.
    6.G.A.1
  2. 28 cm³
    B = 2 × 7/2 = 7 cm²; V = B × 4 = 28 cm³.
    6.G.A.2
  3. 2 faces each of 2×3, 2×6, 3×6; 72 cm²
    Add 2(6) + 2(12) + 2(18) = 72.
    6.G.A.4
  4. 56 m²
    Outer area 80 minus cutout 24 = 56.
    6.G.A.1
  5. Yes; plant heights vary.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  6. Center near 7/2 min; range 3 min; roughly symmetric; no isolated extreme
    The values balance around the middle. Range = 5 - 2.
    6.SP.A.2, 6.SP.A.3
  7. n = 6; minutes; mean 19/2 min
    Sum = 57; mean = 57 ÷ 6 = 19/2.
    6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c
  8. Median 14 min
    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (12 + 16) ÷ 2 = 14.
    6.SP.B.5c
  9. Q1 11, Q3 14; IQR 3 min; range 9 min
    Lower half [8, 11, 11] has median 11; upper half [14, 14, 17] has median 14. Subtract Q1 from Q3.
    6.SP.A.3, 6.SP.B.5c
  10. Mean 12 min; MAD 10/3 min
    Distances: 6, 2, 2, 2, 2, 6; sum 20; divide by 6 = 10/3.
    6.SP.B.5c
  11. Mean 13 min and MAD 5 min; roughly symmetric data
    Mean = 13, median = 13, IQR = 16 - 10 = 6, MAD = 5. Accept another justified choice tied to shape and purpose.
    6.SP.B.5d
  12. 7: 1 dot; 13: 2 dots; 19: 2 dots; 25: 1 dot; n = 6
    Each observation contributes one dot; stack repeated values at their number-line position.
    6.SP.B.4
  13. Frequencies 3, 2, 3, 2
    Count every value once. Bars touch and have equal width; horizontal axis is minutes, vertical axis is frequency.
    6.SP.B.4
  14. (6, 7, 15/2, 9, 18); IQR 2 min
    Box runs from 7 to 9, median line at 15/2, whiskers to 6 and 18.
    6.SP.B.4, 6.SP.B.5c
  15. A median 19/2, B median 23/2 min; both IQR 2 min. B is typically 2 min higher; equal middle-half variability.
    Adding 2 shifts all values and the median by 2, but does not change Q3 - Q1.
    6.SP.A.2, 6.SP.B.5d
  16. 6 observations of reading time in minutes. Median 9/2 min, IQR 2 min; right-skewed with a high value 15 min, so median/IQR resist that value.
    Calculate the center and spread before interpreting them in context. Other accurate, justified summaries are acceptable.
    6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c, 6.SP.B.5d
  17. Example: On a 1 to 5 scale, how do students in our class rate the school library? Ratings vary among students; the numerical scale must be defined.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  18. Center near 9/2 min; range 12 min; right-skewed with high value 15 min
    The values cluster low with a long high tail. Range = 15 - 3.
    6.SP.A.2, 6.SP.A.3
  19. n = 6; minutes; mean 31/6 min; new mean 37/7 min
    Sum = 31; mean = 31 ÷ 6 = 31/6. New sum = 37; divide by 7.
    6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c
  20. Median 7/2 min; mean 31/6 min; the high value pulls the mean upward
    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (3 + 4) ÷ 2 = 7/2.
    6.SP.B.5c

Day 136 — Unit 10 Final Applications and Review

Lesson 1 Designing a School Garden

Standards: 6.G.A.3, 6.G.A.1

Learning target: I can use coordinates and polygon areas to plan a garden.

Read the requested quantity first and identify the relevant model. Plot vertices in order and connect them, including the last to the first. Equal x-values make a vertical side; equal y-values make a horizontal side. Count or subtract coordinates to get the side lengths. Explain why the units and the final value fit the situation.

Worked example

Sketch a triangle with base 4 cm and perpendicular height 6 cm. Find its area.

12 cm² A = bh/2 = 4 × 6 ÷ 2 = 12. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

Worked example

A trapezoid has parallel bases 12 m and 22 m and perpendicular height 4 m. Sketch it and find its area.

68 m² A = (12 + 22) × 4 ÷ 2 = 68.

  1. Plot and connect (0,0), (10,0), (10,9), (0,9). Find the side lengths and area.
  2. Sketch a triangle with base 12 cm and perpendicular height 2 cm. Find its area.
  3. An L-shaped garden is a 12 m by 9 m rectangle with an 8 m by 5 m corner rectangle removed. Sketch and find the garden area.
  4. A trapezoid has parallel bases 10 m and 22 m and perpendicular height 4 m. Sketch it and find its area.
  5. Plot and connect (-2,-3), (5,-3), (5,5), (-2,5). Find the side lengths and area.
  6. Sketch a triangle with base 6 cm and perpendicular height 7 cm. Find its area.
  7. A 10 m by 9 m rectangle has a right-triangle corner removed with perpendicular legs 6 m and 3 m. Sketch the remaining polygon and find its area.
  8. A trapezoid has parallel bases 10 m and 16 m and perpendicular height 2 m. Sketch it and find its area.
  9. Plot and connect (-7,-6), (3,-6), (3,3), (-7,3). Find the side lengths and area. Find the perimeter too.
  10. Sketch a triangle with base 14 cm and perpendicular height 7/2 cm. Find its area. Explain why the slanted side is not needed.
Teacher answer key
  1. Sides 10 and 9; area 90 square units
    Horizontal length 10 - (0) = 10; vertical length 9 - (0) = 9; area = 10 × 9.
    6.G.A.3, 6.G.A.1
  2. 12 cm²
    A = bh/2 = 12 × 2 ÷ 2 = 12. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.
    6.G.A.1
  3. 68 m²
    Outer area 108 minus cutout 40 = 68.
    6.G.A.1
  4. 64 m²
    A = (10 + 22) × 4 ÷ 2 = 64.
    6.G.A.1
  5. Sides 7 and 8; area 56 square units
    Horizontal length 5 - (-2) = 7; vertical length 5 - (-3) = 8; area = 7 × 8.
    6.G.A.3, 6.G.A.1
  6. 21 cm²
    A = bh/2 = 6 × 7 ÷ 2 = 21. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.
    6.G.A.1
  7. 81 m²
    Whole rectangle 90; remove triangle 9; difference 81.
    6.G.A.1
  8. 26 m²
    A = (10 + 16) × 2 ÷ 2 = 26.
    6.G.A.1
  9. Sides 10 and 9; area 90 square units; perimeter 38 units
    Horizontal length 3 - (-7) = 10; vertical length 3 - (-6) = 9; area = 10 × 9.
    6.G.A.3, 6.G.A.1
  10. 49/2 cm²
    A = bh/2 = 14 × 7/2 ÷ 2 = 49/2. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.
    6.G.A.1

Day 137 — Unit 10 Final Applications and Review

Lesson 2 Planning Storage and Packaging

Standards: 6.G.A.2, 6.G.A.4

Learning target: I can connect volume and nets to compare storage designs.

Read the requested quantity first and identify the relevant model. A rectangular prism has volume V = lwh = Bh, where B is base area. Fractional edge lengths still multiply. Use cubic units; surface area and volume measure different attributes. Explain why the units and the final value fit the situation.

Worked example

Fill a prism 3/2 × 2 × 1 units with cubes of edge 1/2 unit. How many cubes fit? Use their volumes to verify lwh.

24 cubes; each 1/8 cubic unit; prism 3 cubic units Counts along edges: 3, 4, 2. Product 24; 24 × 1/8 = 3, also lwh.

Worked example

Draw a net for a triangular prism. Its two congruent right-triangle bases have legs 3 cm and 4 cm and hypotenuse 5 cm; prism length is 8 cm. Label all faces and find surface area.

2 triangles (area 6 each), rectangles 3×8, 4×8, 5×8; SA 108 cm² Triangle pair: 12 cm²; rectangles: (3+4+5) × 8 = 96; total 108.

  1. A rectangular prism has edges 7, 2, and 6 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.
  2. Fill a prism 5/2 × 7/2 × 2 units with cubes of edge 1/2 unit. How many cubes fit? Use their volumes to verify lwh.
  3. Draw a net for a closed 8 × 3 × 5 cm rectangular prism. Label the six faces and calculate total area.
  4. A 5 cm by 6 cm base and 5 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  5. A square pyramid has base edge 10 cm. Each triangular face has perpendicular face height 12 cm. Draw a net and calculate total surface area.
  6. A rectangular prism has edges 4, 1 1/2, and 3 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.
  7. Fill a prism 2/3 × 1 × 2 units with cubes of edge 1/3 unit. How many cubes fit? Use their volumes to verify lwh.
  8. Draw a net for a triangular prism. Its two congruent right-triangle bases have legs 3 cm and 4 cm and hypotenuse 5 cm; prism length is 2 cm. Label all faces and find surface area.
  9. A 8 cm by 7 cm base and 6 cm height form an open-top box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  10. A square pyramid has base edge 6 cm. Each triangular face has perpendicular face height 10 cm. Draw a net and calculate total surface area. Explain why the vertical pyramid height is not needed.
Teacher answer key
  1. 84 cm³
    B = 7 × 2 = 14 cm²; V = B × 6 = 84 cm³.
    6.G.A.2
  2. 140 cubes; each 1/8 cubic unit; prism 35/2 cubic units
    Counts along edges: 5, 7, 4. Product 140; 140 × 1/8 = 35/2, also lwh.
    6.G.A.2
  3. 2 faces each of 8×3, 8×5, 3×5; 158 cm²
    Add 2(24) + 2(40) + 2(15) = 158.
    6.G.A.4
  4. 170 cm²
    Closed surface area = 2(30+25+30) = 170.
    6.G.A.4
  5. 340 cm²
    Base = 100; each triangle = 10 × 12 ÷ 2 = 60; add four triangles to get 340.
    6.G.A.4
  6. 18 cm³
    B = 4 × 3/2 = 6 cm²; V = B × 3 = 18 cm³.
    6.G.A.2
  7. 36 cubes; each 1/27 cubic unit; prism 4/3 cubic units
    Counts along edges: 2, 3, 6. Product 36; 36 × 1/27 = 4/3, also lwh.
    6.G.A.2
  8. 2 triangles (area 6 each), rectangles 3×2, 4×2, 5×2; SA 36 cm²
    Triangle pair: 12 cm²; rectangles: (3+4+5) × 2 = 24; total 36.
    6.G.A.4
  9. 236 cm²
    Closed surface area = 2(56+48+42) = 292. Remove the top area 56: 236.
    6.G.A.4
  10. 156 cm²
    Base = 36; each triangle = 6 × 10 ÷ 2 = 30; add four triangles to get 156.
    6.G.A.4

Day 138 — Unit 10 Final Applications and Review

Lesson 3 Reporting Class Reading Data

Standards: 6.SP.A.1, 6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c, 6.SP.A.3, 6.SP.B.4, 6.SP.B.5d

Learning target: I can represent and summarize a collection of reading data.

Read the requested quantity first and identify the relevant model. A statistical question anticipates different answers in a collection of data. Name the group, the measured attribute, and the expected variation. A question about one fixed fact is not statistical. Explain why the units and the final value fit the situation.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 7, 8. Report n, units, and mean.

n = 6; minutes; mean 13/2 min Sum = 39; mean = 39 ÷ 6 = 13/2.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 6, 7, 7, 8, 9, 18. Find the five-number summary and draw a labeled box plot. State the IQR.

(6, 7, 15/2, 9, 18); IQR 2 min Box runs from 7 to 9, median line at 15/2, whiskers to 6 and 18.

  1. Is “How many pages did each student read last night?” statistical? Explain.
  2. Reading times in minutes are 6, 7, 7, 8, 8, 9. Report n, units, and mean.
  3. Reading times in minutes are 6, 7, 7, 8, 8, 9. Find the median. Show the middle positions.
  4. Reading times in minutes are 2, 8, 8, 14, 14, 20. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.
  5. Reading times in minutes are 3, 8, 8, 13, 13, 18. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.
  6. Reading times in minutes are 5, 10, 10, 15, 15, 20. Draw a labeled dot plot. Check the number of dots.
  7. Daily reading times in minutes: 3, 8, 3, 12, 11, 3, 18, 13, 7, 13. Draw a histogram using bins 0–4, 5–9, 10–14, and 15–19. Label axes. Which interval has the greatest frequency? Include ties.
  8. Reading times in minutes are 4, 5, 5, 6, 7, 16. Find the five-number summary and draw a labeled box plot. State the IQR.
  9. Reading times in minutes are 8, 9, 9, 10, 11, 20. Write a summary with count, attribute, units, center, variability, shape, and a justified choice of measures.
  10. Critique: “A question is statistical whenever it contains numbers.” Give a counterexample and a corrected rule.
Teacher answer key
  1. Yes; pages read vary across students.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1
  2. n = 6; minutes; mean 15/2 min
    Sum = 45; mean = 45 ÷ 6 = 15/2.
    6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c
  3. Median 15/2 min
    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (7 + 8) ÷ 2 = 15/2.
    6.SP.B.5c
  4. Q1 8, Q3 14; IQR 6 min; range 18 min
    Lower half [2, 8, 8] has median 8; upper half [14, 14, 20] has median 14. Subtract Q1 from Q3.
    6.SP.A.3, 6.SP.B.5c
  5. Mean 21/2 min; MAD 25/6 min
    Distances: 15/2, 5/2, 5/2, 5/2, 5/2, 15/2; sum 25; divide by 6 = 25/6.
    6.SP.B.5c
  6. 5: 1 dot; 10: 2 dots; 15: 2 dots; 20: 1 dot; n = 6
    Each observation contributes one dot; stack repeated values at their number-line position.
    6.SP.B.4
  7. Frequencies 3, 2, 4, 1; greatest: 10–14
    Count every value once. Bars touch and have equal width; horizontal axis is minutes, vertical axis is frequency.
    6.SP.B.4
  8. (4, 5, 11/2, 7, 16); IQR 2 min
    Box runs from 5 to 7, median line at 11/2, whiskers to 4 and 16.
    6.SP.B.4, 6.SP.B.5c
  9. 6 observations of reading time in minutes. Median 19/2 min, IQR 2 min; right-skewed with a high value 20 min, so median/IQR resist that value.
    Calculate the center and spread before interpreting them in context. Other accurate, justified summaries are acceptable.
    6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c, 6.SP.B.5d
  10. Counterexample: How many sides does a triangle have? It has a fixed answer. A statistical question anticipates variability in a collection of data.
    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.
    6.SP.A.1

Day 139 — Unit 10 Final Applications and Review

Lesson 4 Connecting Models and Explanations

Standards: 6.G.A.1, 6.G.A.2, 6.G.A.4, 6.SP.A.2, 6.SP.A.3, 6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c, 6.SP.B.4, 6.SP.B.5d

Learning target: I can select models and explain geometric and statistical results.

Read the requested quantity first and identify the relevant model. Two matching triangles form a parallelogram, so A = (base × perpendicular height)/2. A slanted side is not necessarily the height. Label area in square units. Explain why the units and the final value fit the situation.

Worked example

A rectangular prism has edges 2, 3, and 4 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

24 cm³ B = 2 × 3 = 6 cm²; V = B × 4 = 24 cm³.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 3, 4, 4, 5, 6, 15. Find the five-number summary and draw a labeled box plot. State the IQR.

(3, 4, 9/2, 6, 15); IQR 2 min Box runs from 4 to 6, median line at 9/2, whiskers to 3 and 15.

  1. Sketch a triangle with base 16 cm and perpendicular height 5 cm. Find its area.
  2. A rectangular prism has edges 5, 2 1/2, and 2 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.
  3. A 5 cm by 4 cm base and 5 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.
  4. Reading times in minutes are 6, 9, 9, 12, 12, 15. Describe a typical value, range, shape, and any striking value.
  5. Reading times in minutes are 6, 9, 9, 12, 12, 15. Report n, units, and mean.
  6. Reading times in minutes are 8, 13, 13, 18, 18, 23. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.
  7. Reading times in minutes are 6, 7, 7, 8, 9, 18. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations. Interpret MAD in a sentence.
  8. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Find the five-number summary and draw a labeled box plot. State the IQR.
  9. Reading times (min): Group A [6, 7, 7, 8, 9, 18], Group B [8, 9, 9, 10, 11, 20]. Compare median and IQR. Which group reads longer typically? Which is more consistent?
  10. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Write a summary with count, attribute, units, center, variability, shape, and a justified choice of measures.
Teacher answer key
  1. 40 cm²
    A = bh/2 = 16 × 5 ÷ 2 = 40. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.
    6.G.A.1
  2. 25 cm³
    B = 5 × 5/2 = 25/2 cm²; V = B × 2 = 25 cm³.
    6.G.A.2
  3. 130 cm²
    Closed surface area = 2(20+25+20) = 130.
    6.G.A.4
  4. Center near 21/2 min; range 9 min; roughly symmetric; no isolated extreme
    The values balance around the middle. Range = 15 - 6.
    6.SP.A.2, 6.SP.A.3
  5. n = 6; minutes; mean 21/2 min
    Sum = 63; mean = 63 ÷ 6 = 21/2.
    6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c
  6. Q1 13, Q3 18; IQR 5 min; range 15 min
    Lower half [8, 13, 13] has median 13; upper half [18, 18, 23] has median 18. Subtract Q1 from Q3.
    6.SP.A.3, 6.SP.B.5c
  7. Mean 55/6 min; MAD 53/18 min; a typical distance from the mean is 53/18 min
    Distances: 19/6, 13/6, 13/6, 7/6, 1/6, 53/6; sum 53/3; divide by 6 = 53/18.
    6.SP.B.5c
  8. (2, 3, 7/2, 5, 14); IQR 2 min
    Box runs from 3 to 5, median line at 7/2, whiskers to 2 and 14.
    6.SP.B.4, 6.SP.B.5c
  9. A median 15/2, B median 19/2 min; both IQR 2 min. B is typically 2 min higher; equal middle-half variability.
    Adding 2 shifts all values and the median by 2, but does not change Q3 - Q1.
    6.SP.A.2, 6.SP.B.5d
  10. 6 observations of reading time in minutes. Median 7/2 min, IQR 2 min; right-skewed with a high value 14 min, so median/IQR resist that value.
    Calculate the center and spread before interpreting them in context. Other accurate, justified summaries are acceptable.
    6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c, 6.SP.B.5d

Day 140 — Unit 10 Final Applications and Review

Quiz Final Review Quiz

Standards: 6.SP.B.4, 6.G.A.1, 6.G.A.2, 6.SP.B.5c, 6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5d

Learning target: I can apply the skills taught so far, show my reasoning, and check my answers.

The first 1 questions review Unit 9.

  1. Reading times in minutes are 7, 8, 8, 9, 9, 10. Draw a labeled dot plot. Check the number of dots.
  2. A trapezoid has parallel bases 10 m and 14 m and perpendicular height 4 m. Sketch it and find its area.
  3. A rectangular prism has edges 1, 3, and 4 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.
  4. Reading times in minutes are 4, 5, 5, 6, 7, 16. Find the five-number summary and draw a labeled box plot. State the IQR.
  5. Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 8, 17. Write a summary with count, attribute, units, center, variability, shape, and a justified choice of measures.
Teacher answer key
  1. 7: 1 dot; 8: 2 dots; 9: 2 dots; 10: 1 dot; n = 6
    Each observation contributes one dot; stack repeated values at their number-line position.
    6.SP.B.4
  2. 48 m²
    A = (10 + 14) × 4 ÷ 2 = 48.
    6.G.A.1
  3. 12 cm³
    B = 1 × 3 = 3 cm²; V = B × 4 = 12 cm³.
    6.G.A.2
  4. (4, 5, 11/2, 7, 16); IQR 2 min
    Box runs from 5 to 7, median line at 11/2, whiskers to 4 and 16.
    6.SP.B.4, 6.SP.B.5c
  5. 6 observations of reading time in minutes. Median 13/2 min, IQR 2 min; right-skewed with a high value 17 min, so median/IQR resist that value.
    Calculate the center and spread before interpreting them in context. Other accurate, justified summaries are acceptable.
    6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c, 6.SP.B.5d
